                                            PNNL-39086

Juvenile Salmon and Their Habitats in the
Columbia River Estuary: A Review and
Synthesis of Knowledge Development 2000–
2025

August 2026

Nichole K. Sather
Heida L. Diefenderfer
Margaret A. McKeon

Prepared for
the U.S. Department of Energy
under Contract DE-AC05-76RL01830

Pacific Northwest National Laboratory
Richland, Washington 99354

 Abstract
 This is the third Synthesis Memorandum developed for the Columbia Estuary Ecosystem
 Restoration Program (CEERP) on the topic of restoring habitat for juvenile salmonids in the
 Columbia River Estuary (CRE). The restoration program was developed following the first
 Biological Opinion in 2000 to include estuary habitat actions as a Reasonable and Prudent
 Alternative. The preparation of this report coincided with the 25th year of CEERP. The
 geographic scope of the program includes the historical floodplain of the tidally influenced 234
 river kilometers between the northeastern Pacific Ocean and Bonneville Dam. Here we review
 the past 25 years of knowledge development since establishment of the CEERP. The most
 recent Biological Opinion re-stated the scope of these memoranda as follows: “the Action
 Agencies will reevaluate the state of the science, their accomplishments to date, and the effects
 and trends of estuary habitat improvement actions.” Accordingly, in the 25th year of the CEERP,
 we reviewed the body of knowledge developed since its creation. We conducted a systematic
 literature review that captured published scientific articles from 2000–2025 and identified key
 technical reports from research conducted in the CRE with direct relevance to the CEERP.
 Findings are organized around three topics: physical processes, wetland and other shallow-
 water habitats, and juvenile salmon ecology.

 The literature review revealed advances in understanding juvenile salmon habitat functions and
 use of the CRE by numerous stocks across spatiotemporally dynamic physical and ecological
 structures and processes. Characterization of floodplain habitats has advanced considerably,
 particularly structural and dynamic components, plant associations and channel networks,
 seasonal changes, and long-term trends. The extensive research on juvenile salmon has
 demonstrated they occupy and forage in a range of habitat types and conditions from
 floodplains to the mainstem throughout the year. Physical stressors on the ecosystem and fish,
 and the drivers of these stressors, have been more carefully elucidated. The vision, aims, and
 design of restoration projects have advanced together with methods of data collection, analysis,
 and modeling, which have seen substantial improvements. Predictive models are in various
 stages of development. Experiments to inform the design of restoration projects are underway
 or have been completed. The greatly increased understanding of the physical and biological
 components of the CRE ecosystem and its functions for juvenile salmon habitat over the last 25
 years cannot be underestimated.

 We identified three areas of key integrative advances in understanding: spatiotemporal
 complexity, juvenile salmon habitat types and conditions, and long-term ecosystem change. It is
 clear from this review that the spatiotemporal gradients of the ecosystem are distinct for the
 numerous variables that have been the focus of research efforts, e.g., juvenile salmon,
 invertebrates, plants, hydrology, channels, and sediments. This is crucial for both future
 research studies examining multiple factors and for restoration planning, design, and
 engineering. Furthermore, although floodplain wetlands are the target of most restoration, the
 body of evidence suggests that many other habitats in the CRE are important for migrating
 juvenile salmon. Yet, peer-reviewed documentation of restoration outcomes—both habitat
 structures and processes and fish functions—has lagged behind peer-reviewed publications
 advancing fundamental understanding of the ecosystem and salmon habitat functions. Future
 research should emphasize post-restoration investigation to advance understanding of
 restoration success and resilience. Based on our synthesis of knowledge development since
 2000, we identify recommendations for addressing outstanding scientific uncertainties and we
 offer suggestions for program optimization as CEERP enters a new stage of programmatic
 execution.

Abstract ii

 Executive Summary
 This is the third Synthesis Memorandum (memo) developed for the Columbia Estuary
 Ecosystem Restoration Program (CEERP) on the topic of restoring habitat for juvenile
 salmonids in the Columbia River Estuary (CRE). The restoration program was developed
 following the first Biological Opinion to include estuary habitat actions as a Reasonable and
 Prudent Alternative in 2000. Its geographic scope includes the historical floodplain of the tidally
 influenced 234 river kilometers between the northeastern Pacific Ocean and Bonneville Dam.
 Here we review the past 25 years of knowledge development since establishment of the
 CEERP.

 For the third memo, two new interactive tasks were developed to broaden sources of feedback
 and incorporate relevant expertise, which has grown with the program: (1) Expert Regional
 Technical Group (ERTG) review of status and trends monitoring and action effectiveness
 monitoring funded by CEERP; and (2) a workshop including representatives of the action
 agencies [AAs]—Bonneville Power Administration (BPA) and the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers
 (USACE)—and the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA), other major
 research agencies contributing to CEERP, and sponsors who implement CEERP restoration
 actions (Section 2.2.1, Section 5.1.2). We also synthesized more than three dozen recent
 recommendations from independent peer review committees including ERTG, the Independent
 Scientific Review Panel (ISRP), and the second synthesis memorandum (Section 5.1.1).

 The structure of standard documentation produced by the restoration program has evolved
 substantially. Today, as per agreements between the AAs and NOAA, a separate Restoration
 and Monitoring Plan, including a restoration action effectiveness update, is developed and
 delivered annually. The latest 2020 NOAA Biological Opinion reiterated the scope of the
 memoranda, stating “the Action Agencies will reevaluate the state of the science, their
 accomplishments to date, and the effects and trends of estuary habitat improvement actions.”
 Therefore, the cumulative restoration actions and record of effectiveness monitoring of habitat
 benefits for juvenile salmon were included (Section 4.0). Otherwise, we focused the summary
 and synthesis in this memo on evaluating the state of the science.

 The preparation of this Synthesis Memorandum coincided with the 25th anniversary of the first
 Biological Opinion that included estuary habitat actions as a Reasonable and Prudent
 Alternative. Therefore, with concurrence of the AAs, the authors embarked on a 25-year
 synthesis of knowledge, 2000–2025, instead of the ~5-year cycles of prior synthesis
 memoranda. We conducted a systematic literature review for this time period intended to
 capture all published scientific literature that included the CRE or lower Columbia River and
 estuary (LCRE), as detailed in the methods (Section 2.0). Our final search criteria included a
 combination of Boolean operators and an approach to combine different sets of search terms,
 which yielded 668 records. Of these, we excluded articles not directly relevant to the salmon-
 habitat aims and ecosystem-restoration activities of CEERP. The records were classified by
 groups and assigned to one of the authors, who represent three relevant disciplines, for review.
 Additionally, technical reports, theses, and published journal articles not indexed by Clarivate,
 were included in reported results (i.e., NOAA, USGS, USACE, and PNNL reports) (Section 2.2).

 The three primary focus areas by which this report is structured were physical processes,
 wetland and other shallow-water habitats, and juvenile salmon ecology. The review (Sections
 3.1–3.4) identified substantive advances in understanding salmon habitat functions through
 dynamic physical, biological, and ecological processes, and the use of CRE habitats by

Executive Summary iii

 numerous stocks of juvenile salmon. It is clear from this review that spatiotemporal gradients
 differ for the numerous response variables relevant to juvenile salmon, invertebrates, plants,
 hydrology, channels, and sediments. This is a crucial factor relevant to statistical design and
 sampling stratification of future research studies and for restoration planning, design, and
 engineering. The characterization of the structural and dynamic components of floodplain
 habitat including plant associations and channel networks has advanced considerably, together
 with the understanding of seasonal changes and long-term trends. Stressors on the ecosystem
 and fish, and the drivers of these stressors, have been more carefully elucidated. The vision,
 aims, and design of restoration projects have advanced together with methods of data
 collection, analysis, and modeling. Predictive models are in various stages of development.
 Experiments intended to inform the design of restoration projects are underway or have been
 completed.

 Successful outcomes associated with habitat restoration rely on understanding interactions
 between trends in environmental drivers, environmental conditions, and biological responses.
 The three areas of research summarized in Section 3.4 were also examined collectively, and we
 identified three areas of key integrative advances: spatiotemporal complexity, juvenile salmon
 habitat types and conditions, and long-term ecosystem change (Section 3.4.4). Research has
 demonstrated that many habitat types in the CRE ecosystem play an important role in the early
 life history of salmon. Yet, peer-reviewed documentation of restoration outcomes for fish and
 habitat functions has lagged behind publications that have advanced the fundamental
 understanding of the ecosystem and salmon habitat functions. Future research should
 emphasize evaluating and ensuring restoration success and resilience in the context of high
 spatiotemporal variation and measurable long-term trends in the factors controlling ecosystem
 processes, structures, and functions.

 Following the review, we synthesized needs in the areas of scientific research and program
 management. Recommended actions for the following scientific uncertainties and needs are
 provided in Section 5.2:
 • adherence to robust study designs and analytical approaches;
 • juvenile salmon condition, growth, and survival;
 • predicting effects of ecosystem drivers on resilience at estuary, reach, and restoration
 site scales; and
 • quantitative ecological restoration research and modeling.

 As the CEERP program enters into the next phase of knowledge development after 25 years,
 new examinations of program design, expectations, planning and prioritization are worthy of
 consideration. Recommended actions for the following programmatic needs that arose from this
 review are provided in Section 5.3:
 • aligning the future RME framework with the state of the science;
 • updated weighting for the prioritization of restoration project proposals;
 • strategic planning to support evaluation of program outcomes; and
 • data preservation, recovery, and accessibility.

Executive Summary iv

Boxes xiv

 1.0 Introduction
 As the rate of construction of hydroelectric dams for renewable energy increases across the
 globe, the prediction of effects on coastal and migratory fishes may be informed by examination
 of regions with longstanding impacts. One such region is the Columbia River, U.S.A. Here, we
 review the past 25 years of knowledge development since establishment of the Columbia
 Estuary Ecosystem Restoration Program (CEERP), which covers 234 river kilometers from the
 northeastern coast of the Pacific Ocean to Bonneville Dam. Since its inception, the restoration
 program has been adaptively managed based on new learning that integrates science into
 decision-making (Ebberts et al. 2018; Littles et al. 2022). The program functions as partial
 mitigation for the impacts of a system of 32 federal dams on migratory anadromous fishes, as
 described in the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) Biological Opinion,
 and includes a wide scope of actions that span tidal freshwater and estuarine regions of the
 Columbia River basin (CRB) (National Marine Fisheries Service [NMFS] 2000). This is the first
 synthesis to conduct an in-depth review of the state of the science across three domains –
 physical processes, wetland habitats, and salmon ecology – to understand how the science has
 contributed to CEERP and advanced knowledge relative to habitat restoration and salmon
 recovery.

 The Columbia River Estuary (CRE) (Figure 1) is one of four major estuaries on the North
 American Pacific Coast with extensive brackish and tidal freshwater marshes supported by high
 freshwater outflow, the others being Cook Inlet, AK, the Fraser River Delta at the U.S.-Canada
 border, and San Francisco Bay and Sacramento–San Joaquin Delta. Spanning latitudes 38-59,
 all four river systems and others within their range are suffering serious salmon declines.
 Despite recovery efforts, no distinct population segment (DPS) has increased enough to be
 delisted in the U.S. (Ford et al. 2025; Grant et al. 2019, 2021; Hassrick et al. 2022; Lamborn et
 al. 2025; Price et al. 2019). Factors impacting salmon have generally been related to habitat
 loss, overharvest, hatchery management, hydropower development and operation, predation
 pressure, and ocean conditions (Ruckelshaus et al. 2002).

Introduction 1

 Figure 1. The Columbia River Estuary (CRE) is defined here to include both the estuarine
 reaches and the tidal river reaches spanning 234 river kilometers from the Pacific
 Ocean to the head of tide at Bonneville Dam, the lowest dam in the basin. (A) River
 reaches based on hydrology, showing river gages at channel stations (Jay et al.
 2016). (B) Vegetation zones based on reference site monitoring, and long term
 monitoring sites (Borde et al. 2020). (C) The extent of salinity in bottom water and
 surface water. (D) the reaches from the ecosystem classification system (Ramirez
 2019), and the general estuarine and tidal river hydrological extent from Jay et al.
 2016).

 To reach the Pacific Ocean, outmigrating juvenile salmon from across the 724,025 km 2
 Columbia Basin (Stanford et al. 2023) must traverse the lower Columbia tidal river and estuary,
 as do adult salmon on the return upriver to spawn. This free-flowing 234 km reach from
 Bonneville Dam to the mouth spans the dynamic environmental transition from fluvial freshwater
 to tidal saltwater and provides vital foraging, refuge, and acclimation conditions for salmonids
 before they enter the open ocean (Jay et al. 2016; Bottom et al. 2025). Through the last 50
 years of research, the CRE has been conceptually subdivided into regions, zones, and reaches
 according to many different scientific criteria for research study design or other assessments
 and environmental restoration and management (Tabor 1976b; Thomas 1983, 1984; Sherwood
 et al. 1990; Christy and Putera 1993; LCREP/LCFRB 2004; Simenstad et al. 2011, 2015; Jay et
 al. 2016; Borde et al. 2020). The CRE has undergone extensive changes since the mid-1800s,
 including construction of large-scale projects such as the South Jetty, the Federal Columbia
 River Power System (FCRPS), and the Federal Navigation Channel.

Introduction 2

 The knowledge of salmon decline in the CRB that contributed to the NMFS (2000) Biological
 Opinion under the Endangered Species Act of 1973 may be viewed through three distinct, yet
 inter-related topic areas: physical processes, wetland and shallow-water habitat areas, and fish
 ecology. Given their disciplinary differences, studies in each of these areas, within the CRE,
 have tended to be carried out by separate research teams, although a few large studies have
 included principal investigators with different domain expertise. Previous to this synthesis effort,
 two 5-year syntheses were conducted (Johnson et al. 2018; Thom et al. 2013). This is the first
 synthesis to integrate knowledge of physical processes, wetland and shallow-water habitat
 areas, and fish ecology in the CRE learned since the Northwest Power and Conservation
 Council expanded the geographic area of habitat restoration in the basin to include the tidal river
 and estuary. Our goal is to evaluate the development of knowledge important for the effective
 restoration of salmon habitat in the CRE, and to that end we begin with a brief synopsis of what
 was known in the year 2000.

 This report reviews three key elements of the CRE and their interdependencies: physical
 processes, wetland and shallow-water habitats, and juvenile salmon ecology. The review is
 structured around key questions that have driven research and management decisions through
 the last 25 years. Fundamentally, we synthesize the knowledge developed since CEERP began
 and evaluate this to inform future research, monitoring, and evaluation (RME).

 1.1 Establishment and Operation of the Columbia Estuary
 Ecosystem Restoration Program
 The CEERP was established pursuant to findings by NMFS (2000). Recommendations called
 for establishment of an estuarine habitat restoration program aiming to restore 10,000 acres of
 tidal wetland floodplain for juvenile salmon habitat functions (Box 1). A high priority was placed
 on improving access to and quality of chum habitat, particularly in the Grays River. Early
 program documents included an ecosystem assessment (Johnson et al. 2003) and a plan for
 research, monitoring, and evaluation (Johnson et al. 2008). Synthesis was defined as one of six
 overall objectives of research, monitoring, and evaluation under CEERP in 2008. Created to
 provide independent scientific advising, the Expert Regional Technical Group produced a series
 of documents assisting in the scoring and evaluation of restoration projects guided by a
 conceptual ecosystem model, goals, and objectives (Krueger et al. 2017; Hood et al. 2022).

 The CEERP remains under regulatory and operational guidance and monitoring by NMFS and
 the NPCC (Independent Scientific Review Panel [ISRP] 2025). The adaptive management of
 the CEERP is covered in papers (Ebberts et al. 2018; Littles et al. 2022) that describe three
 phases of the program: foundational, emerging, and maturing, the latter of which saw an
 increase in dikes breached and the replacement of culverts and bridges (Figure 2).

 The CEERP is managed by NMFS-designated action agencies, Bonneville Power
 Administration and the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers, which coordinate, fund, and manage
 restoration activities as well as scientific research and monitoring. Five entities (“sponsors”)
 conduct on-the-ground restoration and monitoring, including two non-profit organizations, one
 tribe, one state government, and one federal agency program. Lead agencies engaged in
 scientific research and monitoring to support CEERP include National Oceanic and Atmospheric
 Administration, Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, and U.S. Geological Survey (USGS).
 From the mid-2000s to 2025, restoration project monitoring was led by the sponsors and the
 National Estuary Program’s Lower Columbia Estuary Partnership.

 Figure 2. The acquisition, restoration, and management of floodplain lands by the CEERP

 and levee breaching were both directly allowed by NMFS (2000) and the remaining

 wetland via better management of river flows; reestablishing flow patterns that have
 been altered by causeways; supplementing the nutrient base by importing nutrient-
 rich sediments and large woody debris into the estuary; modifying abundance and
