 1.2 Historical Knowledge of the Columbia River Estuary: A Synopsis
 Here we provide a brief overview of relevant historical research that has occurred in the estuary
 as a baseline for the 2000-2025 in-depth synthesis of literature. This review is organized by the
 three topic areas of interest: physical processes, wetland and shallow water habitats, and fish
 ecology.

 1.2.1 Physical Processes

 Fluvial and estuarine dynamics govern conditions for acclimation and drive wetland habitat
 processes that provide prey and refuge to salmon. Research on physical processes in the CRE
 prior to 2000 was focused on establishing a robust base of fundamental science and formulating
 a system-scale understanding of the complex circulation, salinity, and sediment transport
 dynamics after many decades of anthropogenic alterations to the system (Simenstad et al.

Introduction 6

 1984a,b). Knowledge that emerged from the multi-disciplinary and federally funded Columbia
 River Estuary Data Development Program (CREDDP) from 1978–1984 (with publications
 through the 1990s) provided a detailed history of changes and an extensive understanding of
 system-wide physical processes prior to 2000.

 Circulation in the CRE is highly dynamic and fueled by strong tides at the mouth and discharge
 from Bonneville Dam. These two energy sources give rise to three distinct energetic regions: the
 tidal mouth and lower estuary (river kilometer [rkm] 0–21), tidal-fluvial energy minimum mid-
 estuary (rkm 21–39), and the fluvially dominated upper estuary and tidal river (rkm 39–234; Jay
 1984; Giese and Jay 1989; Jay and Smith 1990). Tidal range decreases rapidly in the upriver
 along-channel direction, while in the tidal river, increased discharge reduces the tidal range and
 increases the water level. There is little influence of discharge on the tidal range or water level in
 the estuary. The flushing time of the estuary is short, on the order of days (Neal 1972).

 The distribution of salinity is important for juvenile salmon smoltification and understanding how
 it impacts circulation is critical to understanding habitat-forming processes and wetland plant
 communities. Observations of low-flow conditions suggested a maximum landward extent of
 salinity intrusion near rkm 45 and a minimum landward extent of ~24 km during high-flow
 conditions (Jay 1984). The lateral distribution of salinity was not observed; however, the
 presence of salt-tolerant plants in fringing bays and wetland below rkm 13 suggested frequently
 saline surface waters (Christy and Putera 1993). Landward transport mechanisms that maintain
 the salinity intrusion also pull marine constituents into the CRE. Unlike classic estuarine
 gravitation circulation driven by a gradual along-channel salinity gradient, up-estuary transport in
 the CRE arises from the interaction of tidal currents with strong vertical and along-channel
 salinity gradients (Jay and Smith 1990; Hamilton 1990).

 Landward transport in combination with the depositional environment of the tidal-fluvial energy
 minimum region give rise to estuarine turbidity maxima (ETM), regions within the estuary with
 elevated suspended sediment concentrations (SSC), first documented in 1971 (Hubbell et al.
 1971). The magnitude, extent, and position of the ETM vary daily and fortnightly with the tides,
 and seasonally with discharge. The most persistent ETM are found in both the North and South
 channels. The magnitudes of their seasonal variations are comparable to semidiurnal and
 fortnightly changes, with maximum SSC exceeding the riverine input by an order of magnitude
 (Gelfenbaum 1983). The observed along-channel excursion of the ETM is roughly 20 km and
 sediment concentrations vary with tidal range. The ETM traps primarily riverine organic material
 from spring to fall (Prahl et al. 1997) and increases the residence time of fine material (Roy et al.
 1981). Material in the ETM may mix into the upper layer and be advected out of estuary or into a
 peripheral bay (Roy et al. 1981).

 The need for a large-scale sediment budget and greater understanding of the fundamental
 processes underlying it were recognized as an important undertaking as regions already
 experiencing significant sea level rise (SLR) began to observe that understanding the complex
 drivers of sediment supply and accretion are vital to wetland management (Baumann et al.
 1984).

 The CRB is the dominant source of sediment (generally finer than medium sand) to the
 energetic CRE, which has currents that can often mobilize most of the available sediment sizes
 (Roy et al. 1981; Sherwood and Creager 1990). Energy sources include wind-waves, tidal
 currents, and river flow and act on timescales ranging from semi-diurnal to seasonal to multi-
 decadal. The relative importance of these transport mechanisms also depends on position
 within the tidal-fluvial gradient, local fetch, and topography. However, sediment delivery and

Introduction 7

 subsequent transport have been impacted by dams, which tend to store coarse sediment
 (Beasley et al. 1986), and flow regulation, which reduces transport capacity.

 Fine sediments (silt and clay) increase downriver and in lateral embayments, and are primarily
 found in suspension, with 80%–90% of delivered material leaving via the plume (Beasley et al.
 1986). Bed sediments are dominated by fine to medium sand and most of the material does not
 leave the estuary. Net bedload transport is landward at depth in the lower estuary and seaward
 in the tidal river and upper estuary, with an intervening convergence zone that moves
 seasonally in response to river flow. Estimates from historical bathymetry indicate that this
 landward transport, in combination with supply from the Columbia River, has led to ~300×106 m3
 of material accumulating in the estuary since 1868, yielding a rapid shoaling rate of 0.5 cm/yr,
 not accounting for dredging (Roy et al. 1981). Together with declining sediment supply, the LCR
 was understood to be a system that retained and accumulated coarse sediment and exported
 fine sediment in suspension, with the exception of material retained in the ETM.

 Finally, while much of the focus was directed toward understanding system-scale physical
 processes, some links to ecology were made. For instance, the increased residence time in the
 ETM allows particle-attached bacteria to form the base of a detrital food web (Crump et al.
 1998). Physical processes related to estuarine circulation and controlling sediment properties
 can determine the productivity of benthic plant assemblages (McIntire and Amspoker 1986).

 1.2.2 Wetland and Shallow Water Habitats

 By the 1980s, researchers across the Pacific Northwest had begun to “use the historical record
 as an aid to salmonid habitat enhancement,” to quote the title of a symposium paper (Sedell and
 Luchessa 1981). This philosophy was leading to a major reexamination of 20th-century riparian
 land and water management practices in the context of their impact on salmonids and it
 continues to guide habitat restoration today.

 Three major studies of CRE wetlands had been completed before the NMFS (2000) Biological
 Opinion. In the 1970s, an assessment for the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers was conducted by
 Oregon State University’s Oregon Cooperative Wildlife Research Unit (McKern 1975; Tabor
 1976a, b). In the CRE, this was a multi-disciplinary inventory focused on fish and wildlife and
 their vegetated wetland habitats, without long-term sampling or data collection on physical
 processes. Among the vegetated habitats identified by Tabor (1976b), marshes were most
 prevalent between the jetties and rkm 127, and from rkm 127 to Bonneville Dam, cottonwood
 trees were the dominant riparian plant (McKern 1975). These first documented systematic,
 quantitative field surveys of vegetation in the CRE were geographically bounded primarily by the
 highways and railroad tracks nearest the river, encompassing only the riparian zone most
 proximal to the river, yet extended all 234 km from the river mouth to Bonneville Dam (Tabor
 1976a, b). One could argue that the study included the habitats closest to migrating juvenile
 salmon in the CRE. Yet the 1970s study area was more limited than the restoration program
 today, which partners with the railroad to restore hydrological connectivity to historical floodplain
 habitats beneath railroad embankments (Smith 2025).

 In the 1980s, the CREDDP (Fox et al. 1984) delineated both historically present and modern
 shallow-water habitats and wetlands and conducted a historical change analysis by plant
 community type (Thomas 1980, 1983, 1984). Primary productivity was also established for
 plants between rkm 64 and the river mouth (Small et al. 1990). Consistent with the plant species
 lists from sites inventoried by Tabor (1976a), researchers concluded that tidal wetland plant
 communities upriver of Tongue Point (at rkm 29 near Astoria, OR) were freshwater while those

Introduction 8

 downriver – including Baker, Youngs, and Trestle Bays – were brackish (Fox et al. 1984).
 Furthermore, researchers documented the role of sediment trapping in plant community
 succession from Lyngbye’s sedge dominated low marsh, to more diverse high marsh, to willow
 and forested swamp, with speculation about the role of nurse logs, and the use of dredged
 material to create islands and convert shallow flats to uplands (Thomas 1983). Additional
 delineation and digitization of historical plant community maps from Puget Island to Portland,
 OR, was developed by the Oregon Natural Heritage Program (ONHP; Graves et al. 1995). This
 work documented high percentages of land conversion that underpins the primary reconnection
 actions of the restoration program today.

 In the 1990s, the historical delineations were extended from rkm 64 to Portland, OR, and an
 inventory from the river mouth to Bonneville was conducted to prioritize conservation areas
 (Christy and Putera 1993). By this time, a coarse-resolution understanding of the elevational
 stratification of wetland plant associations, their relationship to tidal datums, and the continuum
 of plant associations along gradients of environmental change between the river mouth and
 Bonneville Dam had been established. The species composition of plant communities, and the
 associated macrodetritus, only showed salt tolerance to rkm 13 in the CEERP baseline
 condition (Christy and Putera 1993). This may seem to contradict salinity intrusion findings,
 except for the vertical stratification of salt with a freshwater lens at the top level, where wetland
 inundation occurs. Surface water salinity was also significantly predicted net aboveground
 primary production of marshes (MacDonald 1984). A detailed summary of various historical
 plant surveys conducted on the CRE is provided by the inventory report on ONHP field surveys
 of 92 sites selected from a wide variety of historical mapping and photographic sources (Christy
       and Putera 1993).

 Prior to the year 2000, only two intentional estuarine habitat restoration projects had occurred in
 the CRE, at Miller Sands (Clarain et al. 1978) and Trestle Bay (Hinton and Emmett 2000), both
 led by the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers. The former was a dredged material placement site
 and the latter a jetty breach. Therefore, neither reflected the most prevalent post-2000 wetland
 restoration method adopted by CEERP: breaching of dikes on the floodplain that had generally
 been constructed in the late 1800s to early 1900s, to restore floodplain wetlands by
 reconnecting hydrological processes to the Columbia River. Three sites have also been
 documented as historically breached, i.e., dikes that were breached by natural causes in the
 1900s and hence offer opportunities for studies of multi-decadal outcomes: Haven Island
       (2000), Karlson Island (1950), and Fort Clatsop (1960) (Diefenderfer et al. 2016b, 2024).

 1.2.3 Fish Habitat Associations and Foraging Conditions in the CRE

 A comprehensive review of historical research on salmon life history characteristics in the
 estuary was conducted by Bottom et al. (2005), including an analysis of juvenile Chinook
 salmon (Oncorhynchus tshawytscha) size and migration timing originally conducted by Willis
 Rich in the early 1900s (c.f. Rich 1920). In brief, research prior to CEERP demonstrated that
 juvenile salmon were present in the CRE year-round. Peak abundances varied in timing,
 location, and across studies, although peak abundances generally occurred from spring through
 summer (McCabe et al. 1986). Subyearling Chinook salmon in nearshore habitats were smaller
 and demonstrated higher feeding intensities compared to fish sampled from deep-water
 estuarine habitats (McCabe et al. 1986), and salmon consumed a range of diet items such as
 corophiid amphipods, insects, and zooplankton (Dawley et al. 1986; McCabe et al. 1986; Kirn et
 al. 1986). Interestingly, in a study of diets of juvenile salmonids collected from Bonneville Dam,
 Muir and Emmett (1988) noted fish were eating many of the same prey items that occurred in
 the guts of fish sampled in the estuary, suggesting juvenile salmon experienced a continuum of

Introduction 9

 aquatic habitats that provided opportunities for foraging during seaward migration. In addition,
 early research in the CRE indicated a high degree of diet overlap among salmon species –
 Chinook salmon, coho salmon (O. kisutch), sockeye salmon (O. nerka), and steelhead (O.
 mykiss; Muir and Emmett 1988).

 Historical research in the CRE showed that productivity in the benthos was not only key for
 providing a forage base for juvenile salmon, but this productivity provided a foraging base for
 other fish as well. Benthic invertebrates produced in mainstem habitats in the CRE included
 oligochaete, bivalve, insect, and amphipod taxa (McCabe et al. 1997). Invertebrate
 assemblages were most influenced by environmental conditions and physical processes such
 as river discharge, sediment dynamics, and topography of habitats, yet the overlap in taxa
 across spatial gradients provided evidence that assemblages existed as a continuum in the
       CRE (Jones et al. 1990).

 Juvenile salmon prey items were similar to prey items consumed by other fish occupying similar
 habitats in the CRE. For example, corophiid amphipods were a common prey item for multiple
 species of juvenile salmon as well as the American shad (Alosa sapidissima), threespine
 stickleback (Gasterosteus aculeatus), and starry flounder (Platichthys stellatus). Zooplankton
 were also commonly consumed by salmon and non-salmon fishes (McCabe et al. 1983).
 Similarly, white sturgeon in the CRE consumed a range of prey items such as insect larvae and
 pupae, zooplankton, and corophiids (Muir et al. 2000; McCabe et al. 1993), yet there was
 evidence for a mismatch between the most prevalent prey items in the guts of fish and the most
 abundant invertebrates in the benthos (McCabe et al. 1993); this suggested prey selectivity by
 these benthic oriented feeders. Research prior to the onset of CEERP demonstrated the CRE
 provided habitats and supported foraging conditions for juvenile salmonids and a range of other
 fish species.

 1.3 Synthesis Goal and Objectives
 The purpose of this synthesis is to assess the state of the science resulting from 25 years of
 CEERP relevant research conducted in the CRE. Our goal was to evaluate the development of
 knowledge important for the effective restoration of salmon habitat in the CRE. Objectives
 included:
 • Conduct a systematic review of CEERP relevant research in three topic areas: (1)
 physical processes; (2) wetland and shallow-water habitats; and (3) juvenile salmon
 ecology.
 • Synthesize literature review in the context of CEERP relevant research questions
 • Develop new insights into environmental and biological interactions relevant to salmon
 recovery
 • Evaluate a cumulative summary of restoration actions and monitoring accomplished to
 date.
 • Synthesize scientific and programmatic gaps, uncertainties and needs and provide
 recommended actions.

Introduction 10

 2.0 Methods
 This section describes our approach for conducting a systematic literature review, the inclusion
 of material from technical reports, recent prior recommendations by independent experts and
 results from a workshop that convened subject matter experts regarding the status of CEERP,
 and the synthesis of material relative to specific research questions.

 2.1 Literature Review
 A systematic literature review was conducted using Clarivate Web of Science database. The
 topics of interest for CRE relevant research ranged from salmon ecology to physical processes
 and therefore required the use of broad search terms. In contemporary research, the terms
 “lower Columbia River and estuary” or “Columbia River estuary” have been generally applied,
 especially with research projects sponsored by CEERP. However, we noted that research
 programs not directly involved in CEERP and those that occurred before the 2000s used other
 terms. Our final search criteria included a combination of Boolean operators to incorporate sets
 of search terms. The final search result yielded 668 unique records (Table A.1).

 Records were exported into an EndNote library, and Group Sets were used to organize citations
 according to the three focus areas and corresponding research questions (Figure 3). This
 resulted in reviewing 469 abstracts potentially relevant to fish and food web research questions,
 245 abstracts for physical processes, and 207 abstracts for wetlands. The remaining 73 records
 in the EndNote library that were not flagged as part of the Group Sets analysis were reviewed to
 determine relevance to the research questions.

 Most articles excluded were not directly relevant to the salmon-habitat aims and ecosystem-
 restoration activities of CEERP. For example, many mentioned the CRE tangentially to a study
 of another geographic area or mentioned salmon in the context of studying other fish or shellfish
 species. Topics not included in the scope of CEERP through inter-agency agreement were
 excluded from the results of the literature review by the initial selection of key words and later
 reviews of abstracts. Primarily, these included pollutants and predation. Similarly, dredged
 material placement is an active USACE program in the CRE and was not a focus of this review.
 However, because the intersection between USACE sediment management activities and
 CEERP has implications for habitat outcomes, this topic was considered where pertinent
 published data from the CRE were identified.

 While an ecosystem management approach may argue for a broadly inclusive approach, the
 general principle was to constrain this review to those materials closely related to the purview of
 CEERP management decision-making, including future planning, restoration, research,
 monitoring, and evaluation. Factors outside of CEERP management control, including broader
 watershed and ocean policy and impacts, and anthropogenic factors, were intentionally not
 incorporated except where they directly impact tidal floodplain habitat restoration, e.g., sediment
 delivery from the basin.

Methods 11

 Figure 3. A Sankey chart depicting the categorization of initial literature review results for
 further review and interpretation. Gray shading represents the 131 abstracts
 categorized as relevant to both fish/food web and habitat topics.

 2.2 Inclusion of Content Not Captured by Literature Review
 In addition to the systematic review of literature, our review included relevant technical reports,
 theses and dissertations, papers about CEERP published in non-indexed journals, CEERP
 program planning documents, and agency literature repositories from the lower Columbia River
 and estuary (Table A.2). Technical reports subjected to federal agency peer review before their
 release were also included (i.e., NOAA, USGS, USACE, PNNL reports) and were sourced from
 their respective federal agency repositories (Table A.3). By agreement with the AAs, this
 memorandum did not review annual reports from ongoing CEERP-sponsored research, except
 as represented in the peer-reviewed literature. Instead, for this memorandum, the previous 5
 years of Ecosystem Monitoring (EM) and Action Effectiveness Monitoring (AEM) program
 reports were reviewed by subject matter expert members of the ERTG who provided feedback
 via meetings and notes on their findings. We also evaluated programmatic documents that
 provided context for program implementation and the assessment of remaining needs and
 recommendations.

 2.2.1 Recent CEERP Program Feedback

 Aspects of CEERP have undergone review and evaluation by the Independent Scientific Review
 Panel Assessment (c.f. ISRP 2022), the Expert Regional Technical Group (c.f. ERTG 2022a),
 and the 2018 Synthesis Memorandum process (c.f. Johnson et al. 2018). We analyzed
 recommendations from these sources in Section 5.1.1 to create a synthesis relevant to future
 research and program-level opportunities and needs. We evaluated the status of these
 recommendations to identify those that have already been implemented and those that have
 not.

Methods 12

 In addition to the published recommendations reviewed, the CEERP workshop, “CEERP
 Synthesis Memorandum: Research, Monitoring, and Evaluation,” was held in Portland, Oregon
 on June 24–25, 2024. This workshop was intended to support the concurrent development of
 this Synthesis Memorandum, by soliciting the views of experienced professionals actively
 working in the program including several involved since its inception. The goals of the workshop
 were to collaboratively understand the state of the science around the CRE, identify remaining
 knowledge gaps and uncertainties, and assist in the prioritization of future restoration research
 and monitoring. To accomplish this, a diverse group of domain experts with demonstrated
 knowledge and experience working in the CRE was invited to participate. The workshop was
 attended by 26 experts who perform a range of restoration, research, and management-related
 roles at a variety of organizations: federal and state agencies, including action agencies and
 regulators; research institutions; and non-profit organizations. The workshop is described in full
 in a separate report (Jones et al. 2024), and the prioritized list of research questions identified
 by workshop participants is reported in Section 5.1.2.

 2.3 Approach for Synthesizing the Literature
 We used a topic-based framework to organize the range of CEERP knowledge according to (1)
 physical processes; (2) wetland and other shallow-water ecosystems; and (3) juvenile salmon
 ecology in the CRE. Within each subject, research questions guided the interpretation of the
 literature to advance the basis of understanding research outcomes and inform program
 decision-making. These questions were informed by a variety of sources, including hypotheses
 from CEERP-funded research investments, the workshop (Jones et al. 2024), the most recent
 Synthesis Memorandum (Johnson et al. 2018), and uncertainties identified by independent
 reviewers (ERTG 2025a, b).

 The primary focus of this synthesis memo was to provide a comprehensive review of research
 conducted in the CRE from 2000-2025. The majority of the synthesis in Section 3.0 follows this
 criteria. However, during our review, we discovered that in the 1970s, an assessment for the
 U.S. Army Corps of Engineers was conducted by Oregon State University’s Oregon
 Cooperative Wildlife Research Unit (McKern 1975; Tabor 1976a, b). These documents appear
 to have been unknown to researchers who produced the NMFS Biological Opinion and the early
 foundations of CEERP, as they were not reflected in key early framework documents (Bottom et
     al. 2005, 2008; Fresh et al. 2005; Johnson et al. 2003; NMFS 2000; Weitkamp 1994). As a
 result, the program began under the assumption that little to no plant community research or
 surveys of fauna had been conducted on the tidal river floodplain prior to the 1980s. To include
 the results of this discovery proportionately to their relevance, these documents are further
 described in Section 3.2, in contrast to Sections 3.1 and 3.3, which do not synthesize research
 conducted prior to 2000.

Methods 13

 3.0 Synthesis of the Literature
 The following sections include a summary of literature relevant to specific CEERP research
 questions on 1) physical processes important to habitat maintenance and formation; 2) wetland
 and shallow water habitats, set in the context of a changing system; 3) juvenile salmon ecology
 and the effects of habitat restoration on juvenile salmon (Figure 4).

 Figure 4. Measurement methods for physical processes, habitats, and fish and food webs.
 Examples include (a) 1/10 m 2 quadrat for vegetative biomass sampling, (b) a
 vegetation transect across an emergent march emergent marsh, (c) elevation and
 turbidity measurements for sediment suspension monitoring, (d) monitoring feldspar

Synthesis of the Literature 14

 to capture sediment processes, (e) Fyke netting for fish sampling, (f) invertebrate
 sampling for fish food web studies.

 3.1 Physical Processes
 When CEERP was initiated during the early 2000s, important synoptic-scale physical processes
 had been identified and were fairly well-understood. While investigations of system dynamics
 have continued, the period from 2000–2025 has seen a marked effort to understand how the
 CRE arrived at the current state integrating multiple stressors and to some extent, what future
 changes may be predicted. This has involved investigations into the historical state, as well as
 the development of new analytical tools, longer datasets incorporating archival material, and
 major improvements to numerical hydrodynamic models. In addition, a portion of the system
 was altered again in 2010 when the Federal Navigation Channel (FNC) was deepened from
 12.2 m (40 ft) to 13.1 m (43 ft) from the mouth to Portland/Vancouver (roughly rkm 175). We
 now briefly summarize the state of knowledge of physical drivers of wetland and shallow-water
 habitat in the estuary: river flow, coastal tides, water level, water velocity, salinity, and sediment
 transport.

 3.1.1 What Changes to Flow and Water Levels Have Occurred, and How Are
 They Projected to Change in the Future?

       3.1.1.1    River Flows

 The magnitude and timing of Columbia River flows have changed over the past 120 years due
 to flow regulation, irrigation withdrawal, and climate change. Annual average flows have
 declined by approximately 15% (Naik and Jay 2011). Average peak freshet flow has both
 diminished in magnitude by 55% and shifted from mid-June to late May (Naik and Jay 2011),
 and further seasonal shifts toward earlier peaks are ongoing because of changes from seasonal
 snowmelt to rain-on-snow events (Yan et al. 2025). Modern winter flows have increased 25%–
 50% from historical flows (Talke et al. 2020). Changes in air temperature, sea-surface
 temperature, and precipitation patterns due to climate change will continue to affect hydrology
 and streamflow patterns (IPCC 2014; RMJOC 2018; Chen et al. 2019, 2021). Projected
 changes in precipitation patterns indicate an increase in annual precipitation, a decrease in
 summer precipitation in the Coastal Range of western Oregon, and an intensification of
 precipitation during certain winter storms (Salathé et al. 2010; Pierce and Cayan 2025).
 Ongoing hydrological shifts include increased flows during the winter through early spring
 months, an earlier onset of the spring freshet, and diminished flows during late summer (Melillo
 et al. 2014; Tohver et al. 2014; RMJOC 2020; Chegwidden et al. 2020). Rising winter
 temperatures are leading to a higher proportion of precipitation falling as rain rather than snow,
 thereby diminishing snowpack levels. Inland glaciers, which play a critical role in sustaining
 summer baseflows, are predicted to continue their retreat (Moore et al. 2009; O’Connor 2013,
 2025). Collectively, these hydrological changes are projected to increase average flows during
 the fall and winter months (Queen et al. 2021); peak flows occur earlier in the year, and the
 period of summer low flow is extended.

 3.1.1.2 Water Levels

 Spatial, frequency, and harmonic analyses of mainstem and floodplain water levels by Jay et al.
 (2015) refined and quantified dominant forcing mechanisms controlling water levels in each of
 the three energetic zones: the tidal mouth and lower estuary (rkm 0–21), tidal-fluvial energy
 minimum (rkm 21–39), and the fluvially dominated tidal river (Figure 1). In the mainstem, diurnal

Synthesis of the Literature 15

 and semi-diurnal tidal oscillations account for 60% of the variance in the estuary (seaward of
 rkm 87), with overtide (high frequencies generated in shallow areas by the interaction of
 astronomical tides with bottom friction) and influence increasing landward. Jay et al. (2015)
 identified coastal processes as playing a secondary role to tides in controlling water levels
 below rkm 87. This was confirmed by an evaluation of coastal tide gauges that found that
 coastal down-welling events generated subtidal elevations that propagate upriver (MacMahan
 2016). Above rkm 150, river discharge controls water levels, accounting for 70-90% of the
 variance. Overall, locations between rkm 87–107 exhibit the lowest water level variance.
 Compared to the mainstem, floodplain stations are shallower and higher friction saps tidal
 energy and distorts the high/low water symmetry. On the floodplain, the duration of low water is
 typically curtailed and in the upper tidal river (above rkm 196) some stations are disconnected
 from the mainstem during the low flow season. Floodplain water levels are highly variable and
 are impacted by elevation, vegetation, and the ratio of tidal to annual flow range.

 The digitization of historical records (e.g., Talke and Jay 2017) has greatly improved our ability
 to quantify long-term trends in mean water level (MWL) and tidal range. Jay et al. (2011) utilized
 digitized historical records from Astoria (dating back to 1925) and Vancouver (dating back to
 1902) to examine long-term tidal changes. Their analysis reveals a notable reduction in MWL,
 lower low water (LLW), and higher high water (HHW), with the decreases being more
 pronounced at higher discharge levels. Importantly, LLW and HHW have become increasingly
 divergent over time, indicating an increase in tidal range. HHW has decreased more slowly than
 LLW because of the competing influence of decreasing MWL and increasing tidal range; these
 two factors work together to decrease LLW. At low river flows, HHW is strongly influenced by
 tidal effects, showing reductions primarily at higher discharge levels. LLW exhibits consistent
 decreases across discharge, with the magnitude of reduction intensifying with increasing
 discharge. The underlying causes of these changes are the increasing coastal tidal range (Jay
 2009) and engineered modifications to the geometry of the system. Hydrodynamic modeling
 efforts (Helaire et al. 2019, 2020) and analysis of digitized tidal records (Talke et al. 2020) found
 that major tidal constituent amplitudes have increased across the system (peaking at rkm 60).
 Increased mainstem channel depth has reduced friction and decreased MWL, while the
 reduction of floodplain area has confined flow and increased velocity and bed stress. At high
 flows the effect of constriction dominates, and MWL is similar or slightly higher than historically.
 The overall result is that for low to normal discharges, the same discharge now produces a
 lower MWL compared to the late 1800s. At Astoria the 5% increase in mean tidal range is
 centered in June and July with a minimum increase in the winter that tracks with similar changes
 in the river hydrograph from historical conditions (Talke et al. 2020).

 Future water levels in the CRE will be determined by changing coastal tides, sea-level rise, and
 changes to basin hydrology. Estimates of SLR in the CRE are confounded by a lack of long tidal
 records and insufficient vertical land motion measurements in a tectonically active subduction
 zone (Miller et al. 2018). The best estimate comes from Talke et al. (2020), which estimated a
       range of relative SLR from 0.06 ± 0.04m (from 1858–1878 to 2000–2018) to 0.25 ± 0.14m (from
 1926–2014) or roughly 0.4–2.8 mm/yr. High emission pathway estimates (Representative
 Concentration Pathway [RCP] 8.5) predict an additional 0.25 m by 2050 and 1.7 m at 2100
 (Sweet et al. 2022). Modeling (Helaire et al. 2020; Pevey et al. 2020) has shown that the effects
 of SLR are most strongly felt in the tidal region (rkm 0–50). The fluvial region landward of rkm
 140 is controlled by changes to river discharge, while the intervening reach (rkm 50–140) is
 influenced by both.

 The threat of sudden changes to CRE water levels exists in the form of coseismic subsidence
 associated with past and potential Cascadia earthquakes (Atwater 1994). Brand et al. (2023)

Synthesis of the Literature 16

 used a model to simulate changes to water levels to the current CRE in the face of a similar
 event. They found an increase in tidal range associated with decreased tidal dampening due to
 bathymetric deepening and consequences for wetland zonation. Liquefaction may also increase
 tidal amplification.

 3.1.2 How Have Sediment Delivery, Distribution, and Long-Term Floodplain
 Accretion Changed Through the Holocene Era?

 Sediment dynamics and floodplain sedimentation rates from the geological record provide
 insight into wetland resilience prior to anthropogenic alterations to the system. Sediment
 dynamics throughout the Holocene era highlight the role of the Columbia River as a major
 source of bedload to the coastal environment. Baker et al. (2010) emphasized the significance
 of late Holocene sedimentation rates, with the onset of salinity intrusion in the early Holocene
 supported by evidence of brackish diatom presence. Peterson et al. (2012, 2013) examined
 widespread tephra deposits, specifically the Mount St. Helens set-P and set-Y tephras dated to
 3.5–4.0 ka and 2.5–3.0 ka respectively, which established late Holocene sedimentation rates in
 the CRE. Net sedimentation rates associated with the set-P tephra were calculated at
 approximately 1–2 mm per year, aligning with the rate of sea level rise during the period
 (Peterson et al. 2011). Peterson et al. (2013) observed higher sedimentation rates in the upper
 reaches of the floodplains relative to downstream areas, indicating a gradual downstream shift
 in floodplain depositional dynamics throughout the late Holocene.

 In fact, long-term patterns of floodplain sedimentation reveal that deposition rates kept pace with
 sea level rise for approximately 8–9k years BP, closely tracking declining rates of sea level rise
 (15–1.5 mm/year) during the same period (Peterson et al. 2013). Bedload throughput to the
 coast is estimated at 5.0–6.1×106 m3 annually, exhibiting an upward trend as sedimentation
 rates declined. Short-term sedimentation rates in floodplains have varied considerably, ranging
 from 0.6–9.5 mm/year in areas like Hayden Island and the Vanport floodplains, and decreasing
 with distance from the Columbia River (Peterson et al. 2014). While long-term floodplain
 sedimentation is predominantly controlled by sea level rise, (geologically) short-term rates are
 influenced by proximity to the main river channel and the overall sediment supply. In the
 estuarine bays, sediment transport was mainly ebb-directed, with the exception of the lees of
 islands and bars and shallow channel margins (Sandbach et al. 2018).

 Pre-historic insights from the geological record provide useful context when considering trends
 over the observational record (late 1800s to the present). The sediment supply in the Columbia
 River system has experienced a significant decline over time, fundamentally altering
 depositional processes in the region. Bottom et al. (2005) observed that the reservoir system
 has reduced sand supply by approximately 80% from the late 1800s, decreasing from an
 estimated 14×106 metric tons per year prior to 1900 to a mere 2.2–3×106 metric tons annually
 since the 1970s. This reduction is strongly correlated with decreased peak flows due to flow
 regulation, as noted by Templeton and Jay (2013), alongside human activities such as sand
 removal. These processes are hypothesized to contribute to a subsequent decrease in water
 levels across the system (Naik and Jay 2011).

 Sediment delivered to the tidal river and estuary is subject to the complex interactions between
 river flow, tidal forcing, and salinity distribution (Fain et al. 2001). Investigations into settling
 velocities suggest that there are four major grain sizes in suspension during sediment transport
 processes (Fain et al. 2001). Hudson et al. (2017) used remotely sensed surface reflectance to
 identify system-wide surface turbidity variations and their drivers. Surface turbidity is
 predominantly sensitive to river discharge and spring-neap tidal cycles, with comparatively

Synthesis of the Literature 17

 weaker correlations to wind and wave energy. Importantly for habitat formation, turbidity levels
 are now observed to peak during winter due to coastal tributary flows, rather than with the
 spring freshet during the onset of the growing season. Additionally, turbidity distributions are
 displaced downstream with increases in tidal range and discharge but can become
 topographically trapped in physical depressions at approximately river kilometers 15–20. Tao
 and Hill (2019) further noted that while the maximum surface reflectance magnitude is strongly
 correlated with discharge rates, the positional shifts of reflectance magnitude are not. Remote
 sensing was able to capture the surface expression of the ETM and for the first time the spatial
 extent and variability were mapped (Hudson et al. 2017; Tao and Hill 2019). These remote
 sensing studies confirm in situ observation by Fain et al. (2001) that the location, speed, and
 maximum trapping efficiency of the ETM vary seasonally with river flow.

 3.2 Wetland and Other Shallow-Water Ecosystems
 The focus of CEERP research on the region between Bonneville Dam and the river mouth has
 since produced a substantial body of knowledge on the tidal river and estuary. Concurrent
 improvements in scientific methods for data collection and analysis are also reflected in the
 literature reviewed. For example, two standard types of assessment of floodplain wetlands that
 were conducted in the CRE prior to 2000 have advanced, and now incorporate new methods
 and a focus on informing both restoration and adaptive management: (1) the spatial delineation
 in geographic information systems (GIS) of sources including aerial imagery, airborne collection
 of light detection and ranging (LiDAR) data (Roegner et al. 2023), and historical charts and
 maps; and (2) building on this data, digital elevation models (DEMs) and associated land cover
 change analysis. Many studies mentioned in this section have used a comprehensive spatial
 dataset as a foundational source: the interpretations of U.S. Coast and Geodetic Survey
 historical data for bathymetry, topography, hydrography, and landcover (1868-1901) made by
 Burke (2009a, b; 2010 a, b). Additionally, protocols for the quantitative monitoring of wetlands
 synthesized early in CEERP for hydrology, water quality, elevation, landscape features, and
 plant species composition and cover including marshes and swamps (Roegner et al. 2009) have
 been used in many studies. Various methods for on-the-ground field surveys of plant
 communities continue to be based on accepted sampling methods appropriate for vegetation
 height and type (e.g., Daubenmire 1959; Canfield 1941; Cottam and Curtis 1956).

 3.2.1 How Has the Area of Shallow-Water Habitat in the Estuary Changed?

 Shallow water habitat area (SWHA) is a hydrogeomorphic metric used to describe river
 floodplains and estuaries, which incorporates the morphology of the Earth’s surface (topography
 and bathymetry) with the dynamics of water surface elevation and extent. By the 1980s, the
 movement patterns of juvenile Chinook salmon in SWHA had been documented for estuarine
 marsh interiors and edges on Vancouver Island and in the Fraser River where the fish used
 channels until they dried up at low tide (Healey 1991). For the CRE, NOAA scientists later
 concluded that among estuarine habitats, the loss of SWHA was the greatest and likely to have
 the most significant impact on juvenile salmon and salmon diversity (Fresh et al. 2005, p. 39).
 The depth criterion for SWHA in the CRE, 0.1–2 m, was published by NOAA (Bottom et al.
 2005). This criterion was used in the first study of SWHA in the CRE, however, the study was
 limited by both its small geographic extent (rkm 50–90) and now-outdated data (particularly a
 digital elevation model with low spatial resolution) (Kukulka and Jay 2003a, b). Since 2000, one
 report and three papers have treated SWHA of the CRE.

 The commonly available measurement methods for all aspects of SWHA improved greatly
 2000–2025. Improvements included the LiDAR data used to develop topography and, with

Synthesis of the Literature 18

 some limitations, bathymetry; and smaller low-cost, long-life sensors and electronic storage to
 log water-pressure data as a proxy for depth. As described in the most recent study of SWHA in
 the CRE (Templeton et al. 2024), the accessibility of data and indices for flow and coastal
 upwelling, and mathematical methods of modeling tidal range and uncertainty also advanced.

 Building on methods in Kukulka and Jay (2003 a, b) using physics-based regression models
 and hypsometry, and modeling three scenarios for historical subsidence behind manmade
 levees using LiDAR data, Templeton et al. (2024) hindcasted daily average SWHA from 1928–
 2004 for each river kilometer (rkm 21–234). With a zero-subsidence assumption, SWHA
 reduction over the period was 55% ± 5%, or 51×105 ha/year with almost all of the impact
 attributed to levee construction. Examining the period of spring freshet outflow alone, SWHA
 reductions averaged 63%, again mostly due to levee construction but with the reduction
 attributable to the hydropower system being 5%–16% depending on the subsidence scenario.
 Thus, influential papers on shallow water habitat area (Kukulka and Jay 2003a, b), re-modeled
 with the benefit of improved data and methods and expanded from the formerly limited river
 reach scale to the estuary, had major conclusions overturned due to technology improvements
 despite the same senior professor overseeing both.

 An intensive spatial data development effort under CEERP, the Landscape Planning Framework
 (Ramirez et al. 2019), also classified and mapped the channel shallows outside of wetlands in a
 geodatabase to support restoration planning. Numerous datasets were incorporated, the earliest
 being the T-sheets and the most recent having been developed for the purposes of CRE
 classification in 2012 (Ramirez et al. 2019). As previously described, available habitat is an
 intersection of river flows (water levels) with the diking profile across the floodplain (

 Figure 5).

Synthesis of the Literature 19

 Figure 5. Examples of typical channel hydrological reconnection, including (a) Dibblee Point, (b)
 Ruby Lake, and (c) Burlington Bottoms reconnection sites.

 3.2.2 How Have the Area of Wetland Habitat and Other Floodplain Land Cover
 Types Changed?

 This section treats the spatial delineation of wetland plant communities and landforms that
 provide juvenile salmon habitat, and change analysis from the historical to the modern
 floodplain. The first government records of land cover surveys in the CRE were maps including
 general categories of vegetation produced by the U.S. Coast Survey (later Coast and Geodetic
 Survey), which finished decades of field work on the CRE near Bonneville in 1901
 (Superintendent of the U.S. Coast Survey 1871, pp. 46–47; Superintendent of the Coast and
 Geodetic Survey 1903, pp. 127–128; Rabbitt 1989). Digitized under CEERP (Burke 2010a,b),
 data from these historical floodplain maps, extending nearly to the head of tide on some
 tributaries, were later used in development of the program’s Landscape Planning Framework
 (Simenstad et al. 2015; Ramirez et al. 2019). Importantly, the T-sheets were also used to help
 delineate the extent of the CEERP program assessment area, that is, the historical floodplain, in
 2012 (J. O’Connor, USGS, personal communication). The principles defining delineation and
 the other sources used, notably both new observations of Holocene alluvial deposits and
 historical flood elevations (U.S. Army Corps of Engineers 1968), were discussed in detail by
       Cannon (2015, pp. 31–33).

 The Landscape Planning Framework, a spatial data tool developed in GIS for CEERP,
 delineated vegetation using distance from the channel as a proxy for potential influence on fish
 habitat conditions, i.e., the herbaceous wetlands within 2 m of a channel, scrub-shrub wetland

Synthesis of the Literature 20

 within 5 m, and deciduous and coniferous forested wetland within 20 m (Ramirez et al. 2019).
 The study aimed to analyze the land cover of all areas historically inundated by tides and floods.
 The total area of landforms on the floodplain was assessed relative to the area of each that was
 isolated from floods as of 2010; key findings included the isolation of about 460 km2 of historical
 floodplain area including about half of floodplain backswamp and floodplain ridge and swale and
 nearly all of volcanogenic delta (Cannon 2015). This thesis also showed that about 62% of
 mapped surge plain area has subsided by an average of 0.9m (range 0.4 to 1.4m) (Cannon
 2015). With respect to the landscape context of floodplain wetland restoration, the analysis of
 remote-sensing data of land-cover change across watersheds in the estuary sub-basin showed
 substantial net forest loss and urbanization in the first six years of CEERP, and different trends
 among the watersheds (Ke et al. 2013).

 Prior to the Biological Opinion (NMFS 2000), two major studies had incorporated the delineation
 of riparian plant communities from aerial photos. Under CEERP, the most comprehensive
 wetland land-cover analysis to date analyzed changes between the U.S. Coast Survey data
 from the 1800s and the National Agriculture Inventory Program (NAIP) aerial imagery from
 2009, documenting 68-70% losses of tidal wetlands and noting anthropogenic conversions from
 tidal to non-tidal wetlands (Marcoe and Pilson 2017). Their spatial land-cover data were
 incorporated with Landscape Planning Framework datasets to inform restoration planning and
 prioritization and to help indicate where restoration may be conducted without impacting major
 infrastructure. Though Tabor (1976b) had delineated the areal extent of plant communities
 including high and low marsh, Sitka spruce, willow, and cottonwood from aerial photo mosaics,
 the relatively short lateral distance from the river that they surveyed means that the results on a
 total-area basis cannot be compared to more recent land-cover analyses such as Thomas
 (1983), Christy and Putera (1993), and Marcoe and Pilson (2017). The geographic scope of the
 three studies differed in other ways as well, with Thomas (1984) going no farther upriver than
 rkm 64, versus Marcoe and Pilson (2017) covering the larger expanse of the historical floodplain
 (Cannon 2015). The planning and prioritization challenges posed by the existence of many
 habitat classification systems were acknowledged in the Northwest Power and Conservation
 Council’s Fish and Wildlife Program Subbasin Plan (LCREP/LCFRB 2004).

 3.2.3 How Are Wetland Assemblages Related to Hydrology?

 Field surveys in 1980 from near the river mouth to the upper end of Puget Island (rkm 64),
 documented 165 species of vascular plants (Thomas 1984) but in 2000, still no research on tidal
 plant communities farther upriver had been published in the primary literature. Under CEERP,
 Borde et al. (2020) reported the results of sampling plant cover in 4,940 1 m × 1 m quadrats at
 50 tidal marshes from the river mouth to rkm 234, identifying 203 plant species. Under CEERP,
 the zones of the estuary have been delineated from models of water level and inundation
 frequency (Jay et al. 2015, 2016). From this vantage point, we can now see that Tabor (1976b)
 intensively sampled 15 estuary sites and 18 sites between rkm 105–209 in the Tidal River
 (including Lower, Middle, and Upper Tidal River zones). In this 1970s study, tidal river sites
 were dominated by black cottonwood (Populus balsamifera ssp. trichocarpa), Oregon ash
 (Fraxinus latifolia), Pacific willow (Salix lucida ssp. Lasiandra) reed canary grass (Phalaris
 arundinacea), or a combination thereof.

 Similarly, in another study under CEERP, 109 plant species were identified across the gradient
 of tidal forested wetlands from Sitka spruce (Picea sitchensis), red alder (Alnus rubra), and
 western red cedar (Thuja plicata) at rkm 42 in the estuary, to the black cottonwood, Oregon ash,
 and Pacific willow in tidal river reaches to rkm 208 (Johnson 2010; Johnson and Simenstad
 2015). Johnson (2010) took an expansive approach including documentation of amphibians,

Synthesis of the Literature 21

 mammals, birds, and insects, with less intensive surveys of plants. Extensive species lists for
 amphibians, mammals, birds, and reptiles were also included in the subbasin plan, together with
 fish species lists, indicating many overlapping shared habitat usages (LCREP/LCFRB 2004).
 Three of the Johnson and Simenstad (2015) sites were within the region described in the
 unpublished papers of David B. Marshall, U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, who recorded notes on
 20 islands between Tongue Point (at rkm 29 near Astoria, OR) and Cathlamet, OR (rkm 64).
 Marshall (as summarized by Tabor 1976a) had successfully captured the elevation-related
 vegetation zonation seen in this area of the estuary today, including low and high marshes
 separated by Mean High Tide, with willows and sedges on slightly higher ground, and trees
 above that including Sitka spruce (Picea sitchensis), cottonwood (Populus balsamifera), and
 willow (Salix spp.).

 Water surface elevation in the tidal river and upper estuary now can be quantitatively linked to
 water surface elevations in wetland channels using the growing-season sum exceedance value
 (SEVg) metric, which is the sum of hourly surface-water depth through the growing season
 (Borde et al. 2011; Diefenderfer et al. 2013; Jay et al. 2016; Borde et al. 2020). CEERP studies
 at reference and ecosystem monitoring sites enabled data collection at sufficient spatiotemporal
 resolution to develop these models of the elevation gradient of wetland plant communities from
 low marsh to tidal forest. This understanding facilitates the design of wetland restoration using
 hydrodynamic modeling to predict inundation at given elevations, and in turn the expected plant
 community, which can inform site preparation and planting. This understanding of the historical
 distribution of floodplain wetlands relative to hydrology enabled the prediction of future plant
 associations after expected natural disturbances such as a Cascadia subduction-zone
 earthquake (Brand et al. 2023). It is also expected to inform modeling of future flows under
 systemic changes caused by operations of the hydropower system, the U.S.-Canada Treaty,
 and changes to basin-scale flows and sea level.

 According to Christy and Putera (1993), Tabor (1976b) remained “the most comprehensive
 document available on the abundance and distribution of habitat types along the river,” with 48
 transects below Bonneville Dam. It is remarkable how congruent the plant community
 delineations are with recent delineations. Inclusion of this 1970s study overlooked during the
 foundations of CEERP may have accelerated the understanding of the ecohydrology of tidal
 freshwater plant communities later developed through its research and monitoring programs.

 3.2.4 How has Invasive Plant Distribution Changed?

 The Restoration Design Challenges (RDC) arm of CEERP recently submitted the outcomes of
 its seven-year reed canary grass control BACI study establishing the effects of herbicide and
 seeding treatments at mid- and high-elevation emergent tidal freshwater marsh restoration sites
 (Borde et al. in revision). No studies of tidal freshwater ecosystems could be identified in the
 literature previously reviewed under RDC (Sinks et al. 2021). This poses a challenge for CEERP
 tidal wetland reconnection aims, because the most prevalent and successful control of reed
 canary grass is accomplished through impoundments, as has been tested using a water control
 structure in nearby Smith and Bybee Wetlands Natural Area in Portland, OR (Jenkins et al.
 2008). With hydrologic reconnection, however, it had been noted that impoundment would
 require low-marsh design elevations, ultimately leading to a reduced proportion of high marshes
 in the CRE (Borde et al. 2020). Grazing has also been tested locally, finding that reed canary
 grass was the dominant plant at both grazed and excluded sites (3- and 13- year grazing
 exclusions) (Kidd and Yeakley 2015). Replication of excluded and grazed sites in this study was
 insufficient to draw management recommendations, but results did pose nuanced questions
 given that the grazed site had both the highest number of native species and the highest

Synthesis of the Literature 22

 number of non-native species among the sites, suggesting a possible role for grazing in
 suppressing competition from reed canary grass (Kidd and Yeakley 2015).

 The Stanford Research Institute (1971, as summarized in Tabor 1976a) had observed that the
 currently most prevalent and problematic invasive plant, reed canary grass (Phalaris
 arundinacea) (see Sinks et al. 2021) was by then well established between the Interstate 5
 bridge from Portland, OR to Vancouver, WA, and Bonneville Dam, over a half-century ago.

 3.2.5 What has been Learned about Passive versus Active Tidal Wetland
 Restoration?

 Plants

 The importance of addressing watershed scale effects, such as the aquatic transport of viable
 propagules, is critical to attain invasive species control, as described in the literature review of
 reed canary grass by Sinks et al. (2021). In studies of an 11-site chronosequence, Kidd (2017)
 confirmed that 3-6 years after tidal reconnection, high marsh elevations remained dominated by
 non-native invasive reed canary grass and common rush (Juncus effusus L. ssp. Effusus);
 furthermore, non-native seeds outnumbered native seeds in the seed bank and germinated
 more readily under ~ 1 hr per day of inundation. Purple loosestrife (Lythrum salicaria L.), also
 mentioned by Christy and Putera (1993) as a priority for control, also has been studied in the
 CEERP era (Ferrarese and Garano 2010, 2011; Yeates et al. 2012). Evidence is emerging from
 watersheds in the Salish Sea that invasive plants on the river floodplain may inhibit the
 recruitment of tree seedlings and hence truncate the future supply of wood to channels to
 provide structure for salmon habitat, though areas observed were above the head of tide (Morris
 2026). The need for active management of invasive plants on the CRE floodplain status is
 confirmed by the fact that reed canary grass is the plant with the highest percent cover in both
 managed and reference wetlands in the Willamette Valley tributary and the CRE valley (Evans-
       Peters et al. 2012, Borde et al. 2020).

 Wetland Channels

 Early in CEERP, understanding of the extent to which historical wetland channels would
 respond to the restoration activity of notching the dike at their outlets was undeveloped.
 Examples from the outer coast such as Coats et al. (1995) were subject to relatively lesser river
 influence and greater tidal influence than the CRE and therefore were not taken as necessarily
 indicative of projected outcomes. The comparison of notching dikes versus dike removal was a
 topic of discussion among practitioners. CREST tried notching relict dikes at the mouths of
 historical channels on Karlsen Island in the late 2000s (Personal Communication, Allan Whiting,
 BPA). A CEERP study generated data using hydrodynamic and GIS models and a statistical
 analysis showing that inundation of the floodplain area could be achieved by notching
 approximately one quarter of the historical channels, particularly in the more tidal parts of a
 tributary (Grays River) (Diefenderfer et al. 2012). However, while such flooding would be
 important for the wetland ecosystem, habitat connectivity for juvenile salmon could
 hypothetically be greater if non-channel areas were connected at high tides by dike removal.

 The ERTG was formed by the AAs in 2009 in response to the NMFS’s 2008 Biological Opinion
 on the operation of the Federal Columbia River Power System and in its first scoring criteria,
 questions related to notching or removing dikes and excavating channels were not specifically
 treated. Rather, guidance was general, prioritizing “unencumbered access to site” and “restoring
 a natural process or landforms” (ERTG 2010a,b). The following year, a revision was released

Synthesis of the Literature 23

 focused on updating the relevant actions. The ERTG Guidance Table summarized that “Actions
 to establish or improve channel habitat conditions…need to be in a channel,” and it prioritized
 as follows:

 • reestablishing historic channels by removing ditches and other artificial drainages and
 excavating new channels or taking other engineering actions to initiate channel
 formation, and

 • actions that result in no or little impediment to natural processes, e.g., flows in and out of
 the restored site (ERTG 2012).

 Sediment Placement

 Separately from CEERP, the Corps dredges the river to maintain navigation, resulting in the
 need for management of the sediment recovered in the CRE, similar to other North American
 coastal systems (Berkowitz et al. 2021; Welch et al. 2016). Although the wetland creation
 technique is not an agreed method under CEERP, the Corps has produced studies in parallel to
 CEERP, which may have implications for future design of enhancement projects. For instance,
 the use of dredged sediment for the purpose of enhancing or creating floodplain ecosystems in
 the context of CEERP ecosystem-restoration aims has been explored in reports to the Corps
 since 2007 (PC Trask & Associates 2007; Philip Williams & Associates, Ltd. and PC Trask
 2010). These have included the development of design guidelines (ESA PWA, Ltd. and PC
 Trask 2011). A recent nationwide review found only one study of the Columbia, in 1977, which
 found that recovery time for benthic infauna after clay and fine silt placement was greater than
 ten months (McQueen et al. 2025; Richardson 1977).

 Generally, the placement of sediment in open water or on land is considered as one impact in a
 multiple stressors framework (Diefenderfer et al. 2024; McQueen et al. 2025; Thom et al. 2011).
 A recent Corps regional sediment management process engaged stakeholders (Littles et al
 2025) and analyzed data from CEERP-designated reference wetlands to evaluate ratios of
 wetland types (Littles et al. 2024). It has been established that many of the CEERP reference
 wetlands have a history of dredged material placement or creation from open water (Borde et al.
 2011). An analysis of data collected at 33 reference sites including those present historically
 and others that were created using dredged material, recently breached, or historically
 breached, did not find significant differences in fish or plant communities; however, channels
 were significantly wider and shallower at dredged material placement sites (Diefenderfer et al.
 2024). Data from dredged material placement sites, including juvenile salmon and the food web,
 were analyzed in recent publications (Sather et al. 2023; 2026) and are discussed in section
 3.3. It is clear the historical and future placement of dredged sediment will continue to interact
 with CEERP restoration projects through ecosystem processes in the CRE.

 3.2.6 How Have Water Temperatures Changed?

 Through CEERP research, the necessity of incorporating predictions of changing temperatures
 into the future prioritization and management of habitat restoration has been conclusively
 documented. Statistical modeling of water temperature data collected at Astoria, OR (rkm 28),
 Vancouver, WA (rkm 175), Warrendale, OR (rkm 226), and Bonneville Dam (rkm 234) utilizing
 archival data found significant increases in the fall and early winter months of 1.0°C ± 0.3°C
 from the mid-19th to mid-20th centuries, and a faster rate of increase from then to the early 21st
 century, totaling ~2.2°C over the 160-year record (Scott et al. 2023). The largest tributary to the
 CRE, the Willamette River, was assessed using a similar statistical model over 140 years,

Synthesis of the Literature 24

 finding that the largest increase in average Tw of 1.3°C ± 0.3°C per century occurred in
 January–February while annual averages were similar, 1.1°C ± 0.2°C per century (Talke et al.
 2023). Spatial heterogeneity is an important factor relative to the effects of such long-term
 changes on juvenile salmon habitats. For instance, it was recently shown that tidal forests on
 the CRE are cooler than their confluences on Grays Bay by 2.0°C (weekly maximum) and 2.5°C
 (weekly median) with gradients from the wetland edge to interior ranging from –0.07 to –0.16
 per 100 m (Buenau et al. 2025). Although CEERP is motivated by the provision of habitat for
 juvenile salmonids, these increases may also become relevant for adult returning to spawn; for
 instance, temperature-staging behaviors of steelhead in the Columbia have been known for
 decades (High et al. 2006).

 3.2.7 Are Coastal Models for Wetland Channel Design Applicable in the CRE?

 The principles for design of tidal channel planform and cross-sectional geometry for West Coast
 wetland restoration have generally fallen into two categories. One is the evaluation of historical
 channel networks, which may be used to help understand flow paths and drainage requirements
 in the context of changes affecting current topography and inundation (reviewed in Williams et
 al. 2002). The second is the examination of hydraulic geometry, also termed channel allometry,
 an approach based on principles of fluvial geomorphology that relates drivers of channel
 geometry such as channel length, tidal prism, and watershed area to products such as cross-
 sectional area and channel-bottom elevation (Coats et al. 1995; Williams et al. 2002). In the
 CRE, Diefenderfer et al. (2008) produced relationships between watershed area, channel
 length, and cross-sectional area at the outlet for three reference and one restored wetland, all
 on the Grays River or Grays Bay, based on their field-surveyed data of tidal forests and a
 restoration site. Hood (2015) applied relationships between wetland area and number of
 channel outlets, derived from field surveys on Puget Sound, to proposed, as-yet
 unimplemented, CRE restoration designs. Perhaps the most in-depth study to date that included
 new field surveys (ESA PWA, Ltd. and PC Trask 2011) was for rkm 61–137 (hydrogeomorphic
 reaches C, D, and E), and incorporated elevation, vegetation, channels, tide gages, and tidal
 prism metrics and ultimately developed design guidelines.

 Both the historical assessment and allometric approaches were used in a recent study of the
 CRE (Diefenderfer et al. 2021a), which utilized data from the Landscape Planning Framework
 (Ramirez et al. 2019; Simenstad et al. 2015) and Columbia River Estuary Ecosystem
 Classification (Simenstad et al. 2011) including digitized information from the 1800s. Due to the
 value of these and other data sources, Diefenderfer et al. (2021a) were able to assess 144
 island and 164 mainland tidal wetlands, documenting the wide range of channel planforms, and
 showing that cross-sectional morphology only conformed to coastal conditions in estuarine
 reaches; linear models for channel perimeter on wetland area were also predictive in the
 estuary but the high degree of variability within and between reaches argued for use of a
 "natural network paradigm" following historical planforms for the design of restored wetland
 channels.

 3.2.8 What is Known About the Spatial Distribution and Accretion of
 Sediments?

 Despite the decreasing sediment supply to the LCR from the basin (Sherwood et al. 1990; Naik
 and Jay 2011), there is little published research examining changes to accretion rates and the
 composition of sediments in CRE floodplain wetlands. In reference wetlands, accretion rates are
 generally positive and on the order of 0.7 cm yr−1 (Diefenderfer et al. 2021a). Current
 unpublished studies (McKeon et al. 2026) have undertaken more accurate measurements of

Synthesis of the Literature 25

 sediment accretion using the surface elevation table and marker horizon method (Callaway et
 al. 2013). On average across the marsh reference sites, the predominant substrate of the
 floodplain and channels is ~55% silt in the top 10 cm (Diefenderfer et al. 2021a). For mid- to
 high-elevation marshes, the very-fine to fine sand fraction is 19%, but in-channel sediment
 fractions differ above and below rkm 39 (Diefenderfer et al. 2021a). This study also found
 positive relationships between organic carbon and elevation, and between organic carbon and
 distance from the mainstem of the Columbia River, and that organic carbon is higher in the
 estuary than the tidal river (Diefenderfer et al. 2021a). During the CEERP period, other studies
 have shown complementary results, in particular the exceedingly high carbon storage in spruce
 swamps and shrub-scrub wetlands, and high storage in marshes (Kauffman et al. 2020).

 3.2.9 What is Known About Microtopography and the Role of Large Wood?

 During the CEERP era, greater understanding of the historical condition of Pacific Northwest
 estuaries has been generated through the spatial analysis of ecohydrological relationships.
 Such analysis has improved on prior mapping of scrub-shrub and forested wetlands by
 identifying the congruence of the elevation with a 50% annual probability of being exceeded by
 the water surface in any given year and the upper extent of estuaries (Brophy et al. 2019). The
 50% annual exceedance probability is also known as the 2-year flood event, because measured
 trends indicate that it was exceeded on average once every two years. It has been mapped for
 the CRE by the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers, and the values are presented by river mile in
 ERTG (2013). Use of the Elevation-Based Estuary Extent Model (EBEEM) generally increased
 the extent of PNW estuaries mapped by earlier methods, and the percentage of estuarine area
 with woody plants also increased because shrub- and forest-dominated tidal wetlands tended to
 occur at elevations between Mean Higher High Water and the 2-year flood (Brophy et al. 2019).

 The Pacific Northwest has been at the forefront of the consideration of the roles of woody plants
 in ecosystems for decades, and during the CEERP era, such research has extended down-
 slope into tidally influenced areas (Section 3.2.3). With regard to the roles of large wood in tidal
 ecosystems, for example, the step-pool morphology previously documented in mountain
 streams was found to exist in tidal channels of Sitka spruce (Picea sitchensis) dominated
 wetlands of the CRE and nurse logs are prevalent (Diefenderfer and Montgomery 2009). The
 North American beaver (Castor canadensis), also creates pools in CRE shrub- and forest-
 dominated wetlands (Diefenderfer and Montgomery 2009). Buried wood emerges from the
 bottom of historical channels upon reconnection through dike breaching (Diefenderfer et al.
 2008; Diefenderfer and Montgomery 2009), and in newly excavated channels at restoration
 sites, and is visible as layers or chunks in 1-m soil cores (HLD, personal observations). The
 relative lack of knowledge about the role of large wood in tidal systems was confirmed by an in-
 depth review (ERTG 2016), and a study design was developed for CEERP (Diefenderfer et al.
 2021b), which has since been implemented at one restoration site and is incorporated in the
 design phases for a second.

 Sound and decayed dead wood, standing and downed, has been measured in a few CRE tidal
 forests (Kauffman et al. 2020). The wood contributes to extensive areas of microtopographic
 relief rarely observed in emergent marshes or diked pastures but measured in a CRE tidal forest
 using LiDAR (Diefenderfer et al. 2008). Restoration projects designing microtopography in the
 CRE have been studied because of the potential utility of mounds for hedging against uncertain
 rates of sea level rise, providing for ecosystem diversity through the establishment of plant
 species with different inundation tolerances, and supporting long-term succession of marshes to
 tidal forests by preparing shrub and tree plantings (Diefenderfer et al. 2016a; 2018).

Synthesis of the Literature 26

 3.3 Juvenile Salmon Ecology in the Columbia River and Estuary
 This section presents research questions aimed at understanding CRE conditions relevant to
 migrating juvenile salmon populations. These questions provide insights into differences among
 life history types and habitats and consider how foraging and environmental conditions translate
 to fish condition and growth.

 The literature review research questions align with the following topics:
 • Fish assemblages in the CRE and interactions with non-native vertebrates and
 invertebrates.
 • Juvenile salmon life history characteristics – e.g., how, when, and to what extent salmon
 use the estuary, genetic stock distribution, and the interactions between juvenile
 hatchery and unmarked salmon and steelhead.
 • Diet and foraging characteristics, growth, and condition of juvenile salmon and
 steelhead.
 • Environmental drivers and biological responses.

 3.3.1 What Fish Communities Occur in the Columbia River and Estuary (CRE)?

 Research on fish assemblages in the CRE has focused on a range of aquatic habitats, including
 deepwater mainstem channel habitats and shallow water habitats, particularly wetland
 channels, off-channels (defined as secondary channels behind islands), floodplain wetlands,
 and nearshore mainstem habitats. Research in deepwater habitats was conducted in the
 mainstem of the CRE at water depths greater than 8 m using purse seines or tow nets
 (Weitkamp et al. 2022, 2015). Nearshore habitats included shorelines of the mainstem and
 channels behind mainstem islands, which are referred to as off-channel habitats. Nearshore
 habitats were typically sampled with beach seines (Roegner et al. 2012; Sather et al. 2016).
 Wetland channels sampled as part of fishery research efforts in the CRE were frequently
 described as tidal channels and had a variety of sizes and characteristics – e.g., small blind
 channels, large distributary channels, channel networks within mainstem islands. These sites
 were typically sampled with fyke nets or beach seines (Sol et al. 2021; Sather et al. 2026). A
 key distinguishing feature of floodplain wetlands was distance and connectivity between the
 channel network sampled and the mainstem CRE. Sampling at these habitats included beach
 seines and block nets (Sol et al. 2019a, b).

 These studies have spanned the CRE gradient and occurred across all hydrogeomorphic
 reaches. Between 2002 and 2016, 86 fish taxa from 17 orders and 30 families were
 documented in the CRE. Anadromous fish captured in the CRE represent five families and 13
 species (Table A.4).

 The primary focus of fishery research in the lower Columbia during the last 25 years has been
 juvenile salmonids, yet in most studies these species typically account for less than 5% of
 catches (Weitkamp et al. 2012; Roegner et al. 2012; Sather et al. 2016; Sol et al. 2021).
 Threespine stickleback are consistently reported as the most numerically dominant fish taxa in
 the CRE, often composing more than 90% of the total catch across all aquatic habitats (Spilseth
       et al. 2011; Roegner et al. 2010, 2012; Sather et al. 2016; Sol et al. 2021; Weitkamp et al.
 2012). Beach seine catches in shallow water habitats are often dominated by small fish
 (<100 mm), and depending on life history variation among species, catches include larval to
 adult life stages. For example, larval Northern pikeminnow recruitment occurs in shallow

Synthesis of the Literature 27

 nearshore habitats (Gadomski et al. 2001) and adult Northern pikeminnow have also been
 detected at the mouths of shallow wetland channels during periods that coincide with juvenile
 outmigration periods, i.e., May-June (Diefenderfer et al. 2011a).

 Trends in fish assemblages across spatial gradients of the Columbia River estuary have differed
 by research study, likely a reflection of the scale at which observations within a given study
 were made. In the lower estuary, fish assemblages included 41 taxa associated with marine and
 estuarine environments (Table A.4). Taxa such as surf smelt (Hypomesus pretiosus) and shiner
 perch (Cymatogaster aggregate) were seasonally abundant in the lowest segments of the
 estuary (Roegner et al. 2012). In these lower reaches of the CRE, fish assemblages near the
 river mouth respond to changes in salinity over short time scales (e.g., hours) as well as
 changes in temperature and river discharge (Weitkamp et al. 2012). The tidal freshwater
 reaches have yielded higher fish abundances compared to the areas closest to the river mouth
 (Bottom et al. 2011). In the tidal freshwater segments of the river (rkm 110–141 and rkm 188–
 202), variation in fish assemblages was best explained by seasonal as opposed to spatial
 gradients. In these reaches, non-salmonid taxa were more abundant during summer and fall
 and lowest during winter and spring (Sather et al. 2016). Fish surveys in emergent wetlands
 (rkm 37–230) noted species diversity was positively correlated with river kilometer, i.e.,
 increased diversity farther from the river mouth (Sol et al. 2021).

 3.3.2 What Is the Status of Non-native Fish and Other Aquatic Organisms in the
 CRE ?

 The change in flow and sediment dynamics associated with the Columbia River hydropower
 system has created favorable habitat conditions for non-native fish, especially in the reservoirs
 above dams. In a survey of fish assemblages, Counihan et al. (2018) found that compared to
 reservoirs above Bonneville Dam, the fish assemblages in the estuary had fewer non-native
 taxa. In our synthesis of fish data from the CRE, we noted an increasing trend of the occurrence
 of non-native fish taxa in the two upper-most zones, with nearly equal native and non-native
 taxa in catches (Table 1).

 In the lower Columbia River and estuary, non-native taxa composed 33% of the total species
 richness (Table A.5). Banded killifish (Fundulus diaphanus) and American shad (A. sapidissima)
 have contributed appreciable portions of total fish catch in shallow water habitats in the CRE
 (Sol et al. 2021; Sather et al. 2016; Spilseth and Simenstad 2011; Bottom et al. 2011; Roegner
 et al. 2012). American shad were the only non-native species captured in deepwater mainstem
 habitats and in all shallow water habitats, across all reaches (Table A.5). Non-native fish from
 the families Centrarchidae (basses, sunfish), Cyprinidae (carp), Fundulidae (banded killifish),
 and Percidae (yellow perch) have been sampled from all hydrogeomorphic zones defined by
 Jay et al. (2016) and were found in most shallow water habitats sampled. Non-native predators
 such as bass and yellow perch are most commonly captured from shallow water habitats as
 juveniles, at life stages that do not typically pose predation threats to juvenile salmon, and while
 adults have also been documented in these habitats, they have been less frequently
 encountered (Sol et al. 2021).

 While a numerical abundance of non-native fish composed up to ~20% of the catch (Sather et
 al. 2016; Weitkamp et al. 2012), there has been almost no research evaluating the impacts of
 non-native fish on the aquatic ecosystem or on their interactions with salmonids. In the CRE, the
 high abundance of non-native fish during June through September means these fish, including
 predatory species, do not coincide with the peak abundances of juvenile salmon (Sol et al.
 2021). That said, many fish species, including salmonids and non-native fish, are captured in

Synthesis of the Literature 28

 the system year-round (Sol et al. 2021; Sather et al. 2016; Roegner et al. 2012), and the aim of
 habitat restoration is to extend the duration of juvenile salmon presence in the CRE to be more
 similar to historical seasonal trends.

 Non-native fish species are hypothesized to impact foraging conditions for native fish through
 increased competition of food resources and increased predation rates upon juvenile salmonids
 and other native fish (Johnson et al. 2018). In addition, the prevalence of American shad in the
 lower Columbia can increase exposure to novel parasites and disease for native fish
 (Hershberger et al. 2010). Although non-native fish are numerically dominant in shallow water
 habitats throughout the estuary, we found only one study that had evaluated their diets. Diet
 overlap among juvenile Chinook salmon, banded killifish, and bluegill ranged from low to
 moderate, with the greatest overlaps occurring between Chinook salmon and banded killifish
       (Sather et al. 2012).
