 Data were compiled from five studies (Weitkamp et al. 2012; Sol et al. 2021, 2019b; Sather et al.
 2016; Bottom et al. 2011) that sampled four habitat types and across each of the five
 hydrogeomorphic reaches. Zones are defined in Jay et al. (2016). Criteria for study inclusion were
 publications that included species lists that corresponded to information about habitats and site
 locations. A full list of fish taxa is available in (Table A.5).

 In addition to non-native fish species, other non-native aquatic organisms have become
 established in habitats throughout the CRE. Investigations have documented the Asian clam
 (Corbicula flumnea) (Robb-Chavez et al. 2022; Henricksen and Bollens 2022; Connely et al.
 2020), and New Zealand mud snails (Potamopyrgus atnipodarum) (Bersine et al. 2008; Hoy et
 al. 2012), three copepod species (Pseudodiaptomus forbesi, Limnoithona tetraspina, and
 Sinocalanus doerri), and one cladoceran (Bosmina coregoni) (Connelly et al. 2020). The
 distribution and abundance of these non-native taxa have varied. For example, in a study of

Synthesis of the Literature 29

 zooplankton assemblages across nearly 200 rkm of the CRE, non-native zooplankton species
 were most prevalent in the tidal freshwater segments (Connelley et al. 2020)

 An investigation of the distribution of New Zealand mud snails (NZMSs) conducted from the
 mouth of the CRE to Bonneville Dam found these organisms in a range of habitats from rkm 0–
 117 (Bersine et al. 2008). NZMSs are considered freshwater gastropods but have adapted to
 survive in high salinity environments, explaining their occupancy of saline habitats in the CRE
 (Hoy et al. 2012). As a major contributor to benthic biomass (Bersine et al. 2008), NZMSs are
 hypothesized to have adverse impacts on native benthic invertebrates; however, Brenneis et al.
 (2010) found no evidence of competition or adverse impacts from high densities of NZMSs on
 native benthic invertebrates in Youngs Bay. Out of more than 1,000 Chinook salmon diets
 analyzed from the mouth to Cathlamet Bay (rkm 59), NZMSs were found in the guts of three fish
       (Bersine et al. 2008).

 In a survey from a more than 400-kilometer segment of the Columbia River (rkm 56–537),
 Robb-Chavez et al. (2022) collected more than 6,000 Asian clams. Asian clams were generally
 more abundant at sites downstream of Bonneville dam and most abundant near the Sandy
 River delta (rkm 193). In the CRE, Asian clams consumed large amounts of diatoms and
 avoided consuming cyanobacteria, a condition that may impact food web dynamics for other
 aquatic organisms (Bolam et al. 2019). Environmental conditions such as temperature and
 percent sand content have been shown to be positively correlated with Asian clam abundance
 (Robb-Chavez et al. 2022; Henricksen and Bollens 2022), while bank slope and organic matter
 concentrations have been negatively correlated with abundance (Robb-Chavez et al. 2022).
 Moreover, the range of dissolved calcium concentrations in the CRE are sufficient to support the
 establishment of Asian clams, indicating this is not a limiting factor constraining establishment of
 these species (Bollens et al. 2021).

 In an 8-year study of zooplankton assemblages in the CRE, Dexter et al. (2015) found invasive
 Asian calanoid copepods, P. forbesi, accounted for more than 20% of the numerical
 zooplankton abundance. Larval Asian clams accounted for more than 12% of the zooplankton
 abundance. Asian copepods were the most abundant plankton in the CRE during late summer
 through fall, periods that coincided with low flow and increased water temperature. These
 copepods can adversely affect phytoplankton and zooplankton assemblages through
 competition for food resources (Dexter et al. 2020b). In addition, Asian copepods and larval
 Asian clams can persist over a broader range of environmental conditions because of their
 tolerance for increased temperatures.

 For example, one study demonstrated that an increased water temperature initially had a
 positive effect on zooplankton assemblages, as measured by increased abundance, but as
 temperatures continued to increase, native taxa declined whereas the invasive taxa did not
 (Dexter et al. 2020a). Another study evaluated the implications of a non-native foraging base for
 fish in the CRE via controlled laboratory experiments. In the study, threespine stickleback,
 Chinook salmon, and Northern pikeminnow were given a selection of native and non-native
 copepods; all fish selected both types of food and some fish selected a higher proportion of
 native over non-native copepods (Adams et al. 2015). Given the abundance of non-native
 copepods at certain time periods, a foraging preference for native copepods by juvenile salmon
 and other fish suggests the possibility for adverse outcomes to fish resulting from a lack of
 preferred diet items (Bowen et al. 2015).

Synthesis of the Literature 30

 3.3.3 How Do Juvenile Salmon Use the CRE?

 Research over the last 25 years has provided evidence for the expression of diverse life history
 strategies by juvenile salmon, measured by conditions such as timing of migration, size of fish,
 genetic stock occurrence, and residence times across a range of CRE habitats. This collective
 body of knowledge has dispelled long-held assumptions that the estuary was not a favorable
 ecosystem and did not offer benefits to juvenile salmon, particularly those originating from
 interior portions of the Columbia basin. The accompanying belief was that the safest strategy to
 support survival was for fish to transit through this system as fast as possible (Weitkamp et al.
 2022). Instead, research has confirmed the CRE is a continuum of habitats and provides
 conditions needed to support juvenile salmon during critical life stages as they transition from
 freshwater to ocean environments (Fresh et al. 2005).

 3.3.3.1 Salmon Distribution and Use of CRE Habitats

 Fishery research has demonstrated the occurrence of six species of Pacific salmon and trout
 across all hydrogeomorphic zones in the CRE (Table A.4). These fish occur in deepwater
 mainstem habitats, nearshore beaches along the mainstem and in off-channel habitats,1 and in
 a diversity of tidal wetlands. Chinook salmon have been the most prevalent juvenile salmonid in
 CRE studies (Table 2), with catches dominated by subyearling size classes that have been
 documented year-round and in a range of habitats (Sather et al. 2016; Weitkamp et al. 2012;
 Roegner et al. 2012, 2016). Peak abundances for subyearling Chinook salmon occur from early
 spring through summer (Roegner et al. 2010, 2012). Some studies report lowest abundances to
 coincide with summer and fall (Sather et al. 2016; Sol et al. 2021), whereas other studies note
 abundances are lowest from fall through early winter (Roegner et al. 2012, 2016). In tidal
 freshwater segments of the CRE, Sather et al. (2016) found that in addition to spring, winter
 months coincided with increased abundance of juvenile Chinook salmon. The size of
 subyearling Chinook salmon increases throughout the year, with fry predominant in catches
 during winter and early spring (Sather et al. 2016; Roegner et al. 2012).

 Despite contributing smaller portions to the overall salmon catch, studies have also provided
 insights into the timing and distribution of species such as coho salmon, chum salmon, and
 steelhead (Table 2). In deepwater mainstem habitats sampled below rkm 17, yearling steelhead
 and coho salmon were notable contributors to the salmon catch, 18% and 19%, respectively
 (Weitkamp et al. 2012). In nearshore mainstem and off-channel habitats sampled from rkm 6 to
 60, chum salmon composed 20% of the salmon catch and all other salmonids contributed 1% or
 less (Roegner et al. 2012). In tidal freshwater habitats from rkm 110–141, coho salmon
 contributed 6% whereas other salmonids contributed 1% or less to the salmon catch. Between
 rkm 188–202 chum and coho salmon composed 14% and 9% of the salmon catch (Sather et al.
 2016). Coho and chum salmon contributed 10% and 3% of the salmon catch in a study of
 emergent wetlands from rkm 37–230 (Sol et al. 2021). Differences in contributions to the
 salmonid catch were likely a reflection of diverse life history expressions in different habitats and
 locations in the CRE, as well as the timing and duration of studies.

 Off-channel habitats are defined as secondary channels behind islands in the Columbia River
 mainstem.

 3.3.3.2 Habitat Associations, Timing, and Size at Entry into the CRE

 Patterns in the timing and abundance of yearling Chinook salmon have yielded both
 consistencies and differences among studies. For example, yearling Chinook salmon
 abundances consistently peaked during spring months in lower segments of the estuary.
 Abundances were highest between March and April at nearshore sites (rkm 6–60; Roegner et
 al. 2012) and during May in deepwater mainstem habitats below rkm 17 (Roegner et al. 2016).
 In the upper segments of the CRE (rkm 110–141 and rkm 118–202), Sather et al. (2016)
 reported two peaks of yearling Chinook salmon abundances; the first occurred between January
 and February and the second between May and June. During winter months, Sather et al.
 (2016) noted substantial co-occurrence of yearling and fry Chinook salmon, and Johnson et al.
 (2015) documented overwintering of yearling Chinook and coho salmon in these habitats. In
 nearshore habitats in the lower estuary (rkm 6–60), yearling Chinook salmon were commonly
 encountered from March to May. Steelhead are uncommon in nearshore habitats, but in
 deepwater mainstem habitats abundance of these fish peaked around May, similar to yearling
 Chinook, and declined by June (Weitkamp et al. 2012).

 Chum salmon are typically encountered as fry (<60 mm fork length, FL) in nearshore habitats
 and reach peak abundance in early spring (Roegner et al. 2012). Larger fingerling-sized chum
 (~61–90 mm FL) salmon have been captured in deepwater habitats, and these sizes have
 accounted for about half of the chum salmon catch in these locations (Roegner et al. 2016).
 Coho salmon captured during surveys of nearshore and deepwater habitats below rkm 17 were
 primarily yearlings and were most abundant in mid-spring (Roegner et al. 2016). These findings
 differ from those in a tidal freshwater segment of the CRE (rkm 188–202), where the majority of
 coho captured were largely subyearlings and, along with juvenile Chinook salmon, were
 captured during every season (Johnson et al. 2011). In a tidally influenced portion of the Grays
 River, a tributary to the CRE, Craig et al. (2014) reported most coho salmon sampled were
 subyearlings and present in the system from late winter through late summer. These fish used a
 diversity of habitats in this system – emergent and forested wetlands, tidal freshwater,
 estuarine, mainstem (Craig et al. 2014); yet there is evidence to suggest that small coho salmon
 (<45 mm FL), particularly compared to other salmonids, disproportionately selected forested
 wetlands over other wetland types (Eaton 2010).

Synthesis of the Literature 32

 Results on differences in salmon size and densities (number of fish per unit area) across CRE
 gradients and between habitats have been mixed. Two studies of salmonids across large spatial
 scales in the CRE have noted there is little evidence to suggest landscape scale effects on
 salmon densities; rather, seasonal changes and corresponding shifts in temperatures have
 been stronger determinants in the abundance and distribution of salmon than spatial effects
 (Sather et al. 2016; Sol et al. 2021). There were also no differences in the size of salmon
 collected across a ~90-rkm segment of tidal freshwater habitats (rkm 110–141 and rkm 188–
 202). These findings differ somewhat from Roegner et al. (2012), who noted that abundances of
 subyearling Chinook salmon were highest in tidal freshwater segments of their study (rkm 58–
 60) and abundances decreased downstream in brackish and estuarine sites. The size of
 subyearling Chinook salmon captured in the estuary were larger than those captured in the
 upper tidal freshwater segments of the study (Roegner et al. 2012).

 Increased salmon densities with increasing distance from the river mouth was noted by Bottom
 et al. (2011) in an investigation of wetland habitat use (rkm 35–101) by salmon. While the mean
 size of salmon was similar across different wetland types (e.g., emergent marsh, forested and
 scrub-shrub, mixed), there was some indication that fish captured in wetlands lower in the river
 were larger than those from wetlands upriver. In a comparison of three habitats – nearshore
 mainstem, off-channel, and wetland channels (rkm 110–141), Sather et al. (2016) found that
 throughout most of the year subyearling Chinook salmon density was highest in off-channel and
 lowest in mainstem habitats. However, densities of Chinook salmon were highest in mainstem
 habitats during summer months, a pattern likely driven by warmer temperatures in the shallower
 off-channel and wetland channels.

 3.3.3.3 Residence Time and Migration Patterns

 Residence time is a key indicator for how juvenile salmon use the system and is correlated with
 a combination of life history characteristics as well as habitat and environmental conditions. In
 the CRE, salmon residence has been documented using otolith techniques (Campbell et al.
 2010), modeling (Morrice et al. 2020), mark-recapture approaches (McNatt et al. 2016; Skalski
 and Townsend 2011), and acoustic telemetry (Harnish et al. 2012; Johnson et al. 2015).
 Through otolith analysis, Campbell et al. (2010) found that juvenile Chinook salmon resided in
 the saline portions of the estuary from 0–76 d and 30–55% of the Chinook salmon sampled had
 residence periods of more than 30 d. There was no relationship between size of fish when they
 entered the estuary and the length of residence (Campbell 2010). By developing an individual-
 based model integrating hydrodynamic and fish swimming models, Morrice et al. (2020) found
 that residence times of Chinook salmon were shorter during periods of high flow. Yearling
 Chinook salmon primarily used the mainstem for migration pathways, but due to a combination
 of environmental factors and swimming behavior, these trends changed in association with the
 complexity of channel networks in the lower portions of the CRE (e.g., Cathlamet Bay) when
 more fish accessed lateral bays. Compared to yearling Chinook salmon, subyearlings had
 longer occupancies in habitats with lower flows. The association with low-flow shallow water
 habitats may enhance access to key foraging resources (Morrice et al. 2020).

 In an analysis of Chinook salmon residence time in emergent marsh habitats at Russian Island
 (rkm 35), McNatt et al. (2016) found that residence time varied with the sampling methodology
 used to track fish. Median residence time for fish released in the marsh using mark-recapture
 techniques was 1–5 d with maximum residence times of 19–34 d. Residence time estimated for
 fish in channels using passive integrated transporter (PIT) arrays was on the order of minutes to
 hours. In their study, the timing of salmon migration into channels was hypothesized to coincide
 with peak periods of insect emergence in the marsh habitats (McNatt et al. 2016). Further

Synthesis of the Literature 33

 upriver, McNatt et al. (2020) used PIT-arrays to document the use of wetland channels at Welch
 Island (rkm 53) and Steamboat Slough (rkm 56) by juvenile and adult Chinook salmon and
 steelhead as well as juvenile coho salmon. The longest residence times ranged between 12–
 25 d, depending on the year, and were exhibited by juvenile fall Chinook salmon. Juvenile
 spring Chinook salmon residence times were only minutes long. The maximum steelhead
 residence times was 1 day (McNatt et al. 2020), which is consistent with other observations of
 their rapid migration through freshwater to marine environments (Daly et al. 2014). Coho
 residence times were a few hours (McNatt et al. 2020).

 To evaluate residence time of fish in off-channel habitats at Cottonwood Island (rkm 113),
 Skalski and Townsend (2011) also use PIT-arrays and mark-recapture techniques (Sather
 2011). Despite the extensive fish marking campaign (e.g., more than 8,000 fish), detection rates
 of marked fish were less than 1%. Nevertheless, this investigation inadvertently detected run-of-
 the-river fish tagged by other studies in the basin. Thirty salmon (Chinook, sockeye, and
 steelhead) were detected in off-channel habitats from spring through summer, with an average
 residence time of 7.6 h. Twenty-two Northern pikeminnow were detected over the same period
 and had an average residence time of 11.5 d. No fish were detected from August through
 December (Skalski and Townsend 2011).

 The use of acoustic tag technology has expanded the understanding of juvenile salmon
 migration pathways and residence times in the CRE. In a study of subyearling and yearling
 Chinook salmon and steelhead, Harnish et al. (2012) found that most downstream migrants,
 from rkm 86–37, primarily used the navigation channel. Fish began using off-channel pathways
 for migration in lower segments of the CRE (rkm 37–3). While the majority of fish did not use off-
 channel migration pathways, those that did had a migration time that was two to three times
 longer, and had higher survival rates compared to fish that migrated in the main navigation
 channel. Migration patterns were similar across the different salmon species examined (Harnish
       et al. 2012).

 Johnson et al. (2015) also used acoustically tagged fish to evaluate the hypothesis that the
 residence time of juvenile salmon in off-channel habitats differed according to their timing of
 migration. During the spring/summer migration period, 91–97% of the 20,000 acoustically
 tagged fish used the mainstem as a migration pathway near the Sandy River delta (rkm 198).
 The 3–9% of yearling and subyearling Chinook salmon and steelhead that used off-channel
 habitats during their downstream migration had median residence times of 2.5 to 3.4 h (Johnson
 et al. 2015). Consistent with observations made downstream in wetland channels by McNatt et
 al. (2020), steelhead had the shortest residence times and subyearling Chinook salmon had the
 longest. These findings were also consistent with previous findings that most fish originating
 from above Bonneville Dam use the mainstem for migration pathways (Harnish et al. 2012).
 Johnson et al. (2015) provided new findings documenting over-wintering by fish in off-channel
 tidal freshwater habitats (rkm 113–198). Median residence times for juvenile Chinook and coho
 salmon ranged from 11.6–25.5 d from winter into early spring (Johnson et al. 2015).

 3.3.4 How Do Different Genetic Stock Groups of Salmon and Steelhead use the
 CRE?

 The diversity of genetic stock groups occupying shallow water habitats in the CRE changes both
 spatially and temporally. Habitats support multiple life history strategies – fish of different sizes
 occupying habitats at different times within and among stock groups. The co-occurrence of
 multiple stock groups in space and time means that habitats support fish originating from
 numerous places in the Columbia River basin (Bottom et al. 2025). From the more than 7,000

Synthesis of the Literature 34

 genetic samples collected from juvenile Chinook salmon in shallow water habitats within the
 CRE, approximately 40% were from the West Cascade fall stock group. Spring Creek group and
 upper Columbia River summer-fall stock groups each contributed about 20% to the total
 (Roegner et al. 2014; Sather et al. 2016; Teel et al. 2014). The remaining stocks each
 contributed less than 10% to the total composition (Table 3).

 In an analysis of genetic stock diversity of Chinook salmon sampled from the lower estuary to
 Bonneville Dam (rkm 7–231), Teel et al. (2014) noted three general landscape-scale trends:
 (1) genetic stock diversity was lowest between rkm 61–119, where most fish originated from
 stock groups in the lower Columbia River; (2) diversity was highest between rkm 140–163,
 where no single stock composed more than 30% of the total; and (3) more than half of the fish
 collected in the upper segments of the CRE (rkm 227–230) were from genetic stock groups that
 originate above Bonneville Dam. Sather et al. (2016) also found genetic stock composition of
 Chinook salmon varied by river reach (rkm 110–141 vs. rkm 188–202); fish captured in the
 upper reaches represented a greater diversity of stock groups than those captured from lower in
 the CRE. The longitudinal variation of genetic stock composition illustrates the diversity and co-
 occurrence of Chinook salmon stock groups in the CRE. Regardless of spatial variation in
 Chinook salmon genetic stocks, genetic stock of origin has not been an important factor related
 to growth of fish captured in the CRE (Goertler et al. 2016b; Chittaro et al. 2018).
