 3.3.4.1 Temporal Distribution of Stock Groups

 Unmarked West Cascade fall-run fish have been documented in the CRE from winter through
 fall months with peak abundances occurring during mid-spring and mid-summer (Teel et al.
 2014). In the lower segments of the CRE (rkm 6–60), this stock group was the most abundant
 and was prevalent in catches during all seasons (Roegner et al. 2012). The prevalence of West
 Cascade fall-run fish throughout the year meant there were few significant differences in stock
 groups among seasons in middle segments of the CRE (rkm 110–141). In upper segments of

Synthesis of the Literature 35

 the CRE (rkm 188–202), where West Cascade fall-run fish were less predominant, the stock
 compositions were remarkably similar across years, and seasonal shifts in stock composition
 were a larger source of variation in the data compared to middle CRE segments (Sather et al.
 2016).

 Between rkm 188–202, winter months were dominated by Spring Creek group Chinook salmon,
 a pattern that was also noted by Roegner et al. 2012 in the lower CRE (rkm 6–60). The
 composition of Chinook salmon during spring has been dominated by Spring Creek group fall
 stock and West Cascade fall-run stocks at rkm 6–60 (Roegner et al. 2012). Willamette River
 spring Chinook salmon have been most prevalent during early spring (Teel et al. 2014) and fall
 (Sather et al. 2016). During spring and summer months, catches of Chinook salmon have
 included large percentages of upper Columbia River summer-fall fish (Sather et al. 2016; Teel et
 al. 2014).

 Similar to unmarked stock groups, genetic stock groups of marked hatchery Chinook salmon
 differ in their seasonal use of CRE habitats. Spring-run hatchery fish from West Cascade spring
 and Willamette River spring stock groups were most abundant during early spring (Teel et al.
 2014). Hatchery Chinook salmon from the Spring Creek group fall stock were prevalent during
 winter and spring months. During summer months, the composition of hatchery fish was
 dominated by the upper Columbia River summer-fall stock (Sather et al. 2016; Teel et al. 2014).

 3.3.4.2 Habitat Use and Comparisons

 Despite seasonal and landscape differences in genetic stock groups, there have not always
 been clear differences when making comparisons across habitat types. Sather et al. (2016)
 evaluated nearshore mainstem, off-channel, and wetland-channel habitats and found no
 differences in genetic stocks of unmarked or marked Chinook salmon between the three habitat
 types. Teel et al. (2014) noted some differences in genetic stock composition between
 nearshore mainstem and off-channel habitats, but these differences varied across the
 landscape and no clear trend in differences among these habitat types was observed. They did,
 however, note that confluence sites yielded the lowest diversity in genetic stock groups of
 salmon – a finding that was attributed to the likelihood that fish captured at confluence locations
 reflected local populations within each of the tributaries (Teel et al. 2014).

 In a comparison of shallow nearshore beach habitats with deepwater mainstem habitats (rkm
 13–19), Roegner et al. (2016) found that while there was some overlap in the occurrence of
 stock groups between the two habitat types, the proportions were different. Nearshore habitats
 were dominated by fall-run Chinook salmon from the lower Columbia River, whereas deepwater
 habitats had a greater diversity of genetic stock groups and higher proportions of interior stocks
 (Roegner et al. 2016). The same findings were also observed in a comparison of subyearling
 Chinook salmon between deepwater mainstem and tidal wetland habitats; higher stock diversity
 and higher proportion of interior stocks in mainstem deepwater habitats (Sather et al. 2020b).
 Regardless of the variation in stock groups across shallow water habitat types, these studies
 have demonstrated that Chinook salmon originating from interior portions of the Columbia River
 and Snake River use the same CRE habitats as Chinook salmon originating from tributaries in
 the lower river.

 Similar to shallow water habitats, patterns in the timing and persitence of salmon and steelhead
 in deepwater habitats vary by genetic stock groups. In a landscape-scale analysis (rkm 15–210)
 of steelhead and yearling Chinook salmon captured in deepwater habitats of the CRE,
 Weitkamp et al. (2022) found that most fish were from interior stock groups. The majority of

Synthesis of the Literature 36

 steelhead sampled along the CRE gradient were from the mid and upper Columbia River/lower
 Snake River and upper Snake River stock groups. Willamette River spring Chinook salmon
 contributed less than 5% to the yearling Chinook salmon catch, and those fish were most
 abundant at the lowest site (rkm 15) (Weitkamp et al. 2022).

 In a study of deepwater habitats in the lower estuary (rkm 13–17), steelhead and yearling
 Chinook salmon originating from interior portions of the Columbia River arrived later than fish
 from tributaries in the lower river. For example, yearling Chinook salmon from the upper
 Columbia River summer-fall stock were collected 30 d after the peak abundance of the
 Willamette River spring stock (Weitkamp et al. 2015). In addition, Chinook salmon from the
 Snake River spring and mid and upper Columbia River spring stock groups were smaller
 compared to other stock groups. Subyearling Chinook salmon in the estuary were encountered
 from spring through summer in deepwater habitats below rkm 17. Spring Creek group fall
 Chinook salmon were the first of the subyearling stock groups to enter the estuary during spring.
 These fish arrived nearly 3 months before the West Cascade fall stock, a group that entered the
 estuary during late summer (Weitkamp et al. 2015). While there are unique aspects to the timing
 of entry and the duration of migration for genetic stock groups in deepwater habitats, there is
 also notable overlap in the occurrence of multiple stock groups. These patterns of genetic stock
 co-occurrence were also noted in shallow water habitats throughout the CRE gradient.

 3.3.5 How Do Hatchery Salmon and Steelhead Use Estuary Habitats, and How
 Does Their Use Differ from Unmarked Fish?

 The timing of hatchery releases has resulted in high spatial overlap between hatchery and non-
 hatchery salmonids in the CRE (Weitkamp et al. 2015; Bottom et al. 2021; Roegner et al. 2016).
 While the proportion of hatchery and unmarked salmonids has varied across studies and
 locations in the CRE, the most notable distinction has been between deepwater and nearshore
 habitats. For example, hatchery fish composed between 80% and 100% of salmon catches in
 deepwater habitats (Weitkamp et al. 2022, 2012). In contrast, studies of salmon in nearshore
 habitats report marked salmon composed approximately 10% to 30% of the salmon catches
 (Roegner et al. 2012; Sather et al. 2016; Bottom et al. 2021). However, the proportions of
 marked fish in nearshore habitats varied spatially across the CRE, and in some locations,
 hatchery Chinook salmon have accounted for more than 30% of the salmon catch (Bottom et al.
 2021). There has also been evidence for a higher proportion of marked fish in nearshore
 mainstem habitats compared to off-channel and wetland channel habitats (Bottom et al. 2021).

 Research across a range of locations and habitat types in the CRE has established that
 hatchery salmon are larger than unmarked salmon (Daly et al. 2014; Bottom et al. 2021;
 Weitkamp et al. 2022, 2012, 2015; Sather et al. 2026). In a study of nearshore habitats from the
 mouth to Bonneville Dam, Bottom et al. (2021) found that while hatchery fish composed ~30%
 of the Chinook salmon catch, they accounted for ~50% of the Chinook salmon biomass. In
 deepwater habitats, hatchery yearling Chinook salmon, coho salmon, and steelhead were
 longer than their unmarked counterparts (Weitkamp et al. 2012, 2015, 2022). Larger fish
 captured in the estuary were found to have originated from hatcheries that released larger-sized
 fish, compared to smaller fish that had originated from hatcheries using release strategies of
 smaller size classes, which is evidence that hatchery rearing practices are detectable in the
 estuary (Weitkamp et al. 2015).

 While hatchery fish are often reported as larger than their unmarked counterparts, Goertler et al.
 (2016a) analyzed otolith microstructures and found these fish had lower variability in growth, a
 pattern that may be influenced by rearing in a controlled hatchery setting. The trends found by

Synthesis of the Literature 37

 this study indicated that growth of hatchery fish was characterized by an early phase of high
 growth followed by a sharp decline, likely coinciding with the time when hatchery fish were
 released (Goertler et al. 2016a). Bottom et al. (2021) also noted larger median sizes of hatchery
 fish in nearshore habitats, yet the variation in size ranges of hatchery fish was narrower
 compared to those of unmarked fish. In a study of emergent wetland habitats from the mouth to
 Bonneville Dam, Chittaro et al. (2018) found hatchery subyearling Chinook salmon were larger
 than unmarked Chinook salmon, yet the growth rates between the two groups were similar.
 These studies have effectively shown that despite the large size advantage of hatchery fish, this
 hasn’t translated into different growth patterns. Hatchery fish also do not exhibit the range of
 size classes of unmarked fish within the estuary. Regardless, larger hatchery fish may be more
 equipped to compete for food resources compared to smaller fish (Weitkamp et al. 2015;
 Bottom et al. 2021). The spatial and temporal overlap between hatchery and unmarked salmon
 and steelhead, along with notable overlap in diet, suggest a need to better understand the
 degree of interaction between these groups of fish (Weitkamp et al. 2022).

 While the assemblages of non-salmon fishes in the estuary have been notably variable, there
 has been remarkable consistency in the timing of salmon abundance in the estuary, a condition
 that is likely attributed to hatchery management practices that focus production on rearing fish to
 a particular size and date (Weitkamp et al. 2012). In deepwater habitats, the similarities in timing
 of entry into the estuary by hatchery and unmarked yearling Chinook salmon and steelhead
 provide a basis to use hatchery fish as a proxy for the less abundant unmarked groups.
 Similarities in timing of estuarine entrance indicate that hatchery fish respond similarly to
 environmental conditions used by unmarked fish (Weitkamp et al. 2015). These assumptions,
 however, do not hold true for hatchery subyearling Chinook salmon that have migrated to the
 estuary nearly 1 month earlier than unmarked Chinook salmon – a pattern likely explained by
 greater variability and longer residency in estuarine habitats compared to yearling life history
 types (Weitkamp et al. 2015). In nearshore habitats, hatchery Chinook salmon were
 predominant in catches during May and July and were nearly absent by September (Bottom et
 al. 2021).

 3.3.6 What Are the Foraging Conditions and Diet Patterns of Juvenile Salmon
 and Steelhead in the CRE?

 Research on foraging ecology of juvenile salmon has spanned the CRE gradient and included
 habitats ranging from deepwater mainstem channel to floodplain (Table A.6). Aquatic and
 terrestrial insects make up appreciable proportions of the diets of juvenile salmon and steelhead
 across a range of habitats and seasons as well as across different size classes and species of
 salmonids (Weitkamp et al. 2022; Accola et al. 2025; Storch and Sather 2011). Amphipods,
 primarily Gammaridae, have also been a notable contributor to the diets of juvenile salmon and
 steelhead, particularly for larger size classes of fish (Weitkamp et al. 2022; Lott 2004; Sather et
 al. 2026). While prey items such as Chironomidae and corophiid amphipods have been
 ubiquitous in the diets of juvenile salmon, studies analyzing the stomach contents of more than
 3,000 Chinook salmon have documented more than 200 unique prey taxa consumed by fish
 migrating in the CRE (Table A.6).2 These findings highlight that despite the prevalence of select
 diet items (e.g., Chironomidae and corophiid amphipods), juvenile salmon are opportunistic
 feeders, adapting their foraging strategy to the environment and their energetic needs (Accola et
 al. 2025; Haskell and Tiffan 2011; Storch and Sather 2011).

 2
 There have been more than eight studies and more than 3,000 diet samples collected from juvenile
 salmon and steelhead in the estuary since CEERP began. The numbers reported here reflect the
 availability of published data detailing gut contents.

Synthesis of the Literature 38

 Research has clearly shown that insects and crustaceans are key prey items for juvenile
 salmonids during their residence and migration through the CRE, yet zooplankton such as
 copepods and cladocerans have been notable contributors to salmon diets at select locations
 and times. For example, copepods and cladocerans made up high portions of subyearling
 Chinook salmon diets in floodplain wetlands (rkm 161; Klopfenstein 2016, and rkm 149; Accola
 et al. 2025), nearshore mainstem habitats (rkm 87; Haskell and Tiffan 2011), and in tidal
 freshwater wetlands (rkm 90–105; Sather et al. 2026).

 Through isotopic analysis, Maier and Simenstad (2009) found that subyearling Chinook salmon
 assimilate a variety of organic matter sources, and while fluvial phytoplankton was a contributor
 at select times and locations, it was less important than sources linked to wetland productivity.
 Invertebrates consumed by juvenile salmon also derived energy from multiple sources that
 shifted through the spring and summer months. For example, Chironomids derived energy
 sources from fluvial phytoplankton and aquatic plants during early spring months. Aquatic and
 vascular plants contributed more to Chironomid energy assimilation from May–June, and
 benthic diatoms became the predominant source of energy during July (Maier and Simenstad,
 2009). Corophiid amphipods have different life history characteristics and habitats associations
 than chironomids, which translates into different patterns of assimilation. For instance, vascular
 plant detritus was a significant source of energy for corophiids collected in tidal freshwater sites
 across all months, yet fluvial phytoplankton was a notable source from April–May. Detritus is
 typically flushed out of estuarine tributary watersheds to downriver habitats or the mainstem
 Columbia River during storms in the fall and winter months (Thom et al. 2018). Corophiids
 collected closer to the Columbia River mouth assimilated energy from marine phytoplankton and
 benthic macroalgae (Maier and Simenstad 2009).

 3.3.6.1 Landscape-Scale Variation: Fish Diets

 The variation in salmon diets across landscape-scale gradients has varied across studies,
 suggesting foraging patterns in the CRE are related to life history variation, habitat associations,
 and study design elements such as the scale at which observations are made. In a study of
 subyearling Chinook salmon captured in emergent wetlands from rkm 6–221, Accola et al.
 (2025) found that diets of fish were most similar between the lowest three sites (rkm 6 to 72),
 where amphipods were common diet items. Dipterans were commonly encountered in fish diets
 at the two uppermost sites (rkm 149 and 221).

 In mainstem habitats, Weitkamp et al. (2022) also noted evidence of a CRE gradient effect with
 differences in diets of yearling Chinook salmon and steelhead being most distinct between the
 lowest (rkm 15) and uppermost (rkm 210) stations. Unlike the Accola et al. (2025) study of fish
 diets in wetlands, amphipods were more abundant in diets of fish from the uppermost mainstem
 sites (Weitkamp et al. 2022), although these crustaceans were found in fish diets throughout the
 entire gradient.

 Across a 65 rkm segment of the CRE, Sather et al. (2026) found that diet biomass was greater
 and contained higher proportions of adult insects and amphipods in lower estuary wetlands (rkm
 43–56) compared to diets of fish sampled from wetlands further upstream (rkm 90–105). The
 lower diet biomass at wetlands sampled upstream was driven by a predominance of
 zooplankton in Chinook salmon diets (Sather et al. 2026).

 While each of these studies have noted different patterns in salmon diets occurring across
 spatial gradients, it is important to note these studies have also reported a notable amount of
 overlap in prey items found in the diets of fish throughout the CRE. Commonalities among

Synthesis of the Literature 39

 foraging patterns by juvenile salmon provide evidence that despite the range of diverse habitats
 across the CRE landscape, there is overlap in the availability of prey taxa among habitats and
 locations.

 3.3.6.2 Habitat-Scale Variation: Fish Diets

 Fish Diets in Wetland Habitats

 Similar to the trends of overlapping diet items across the CRE gradient, studies that have made
 diet comparisons across smaller spatial scales – e.g., between sites or habitats – have also
 reported a notable amount of overlap in diet items. For example, Spilseth and Simenstad (2011)
 sampled Chinook salmon and threespine stickleback diets from an emergent marsh and
 forested scrub-shrub wetlands (rkm 36–85) and the respective diets were not different between
 the habitats sampled. After comparing diets of Chinook salmon captured from three wetland
 types – emergent marsh, scrub-shrub, and forested – Lott (2004) concluded there were few
 differences in diets of fish across these locations and noted that chironomids were a
 predominant prey item in all of the wetlands. There was some indication that consumption rates
 of fish sampled from the emergent marsh were higher than consumption of fish sampled from
 forested wetlands, although differences were not statistically significant. Chinook salmon
 captured in the emergent marsh had higher diversity of diet items and higher stomach fullness
 (Lott 2004). In the tidally influenced portion of the Grays River, differences in diet were more
 prevalent between species of subyearling salmonids – Chinook, coho, and chum – and
 seasonal diet differences were more prevalent than differences in diets between habitat types –
 forested wetland, emergent marshes, and mainstem habitats. Further, there were no differences
 in subyearling salmon feeding intensities between habitats (Eaton 2010).

 Comparisons of Fish Diets in the Mainstem with Other Habitat Types

 In a comparison of Chinook salmon diets sampled from nearshore habitats of the Columbia
 River mainstem (rkm 162) and the Willamette River confluence (rkm 164), and Multnomah
 Channel wetland habitats (rkm 160), Klopfenstein (2016) noted that adult and pupal chironomids
 were common and consumed at similar proportions across the three habitats. However, there
 were differences in the consumption of Americorophium sp., where the highest consumption
 occurred at the mainstem, and lowest consumption occurred from wetland habitats in
 Multnomah channel. In comparing all diet items of Chinook salmon, there were no diet
 differences between fish in the Columbia River mainstem and those sampled from the
 Willamette River confluence. Similarly, there were few differences in fish diets between
 Multnomah Channel and the Willamette River confluence. Diets of fish were most distinct
 between the Columbia River mainstem and wetlands in Multnomah Channel. Fish in the
 wetlands consumed a higher proportion of terrestrial prey and fish in the mainstem consumed
 a higher proportion of benthic prey (Klopfenstein 2016).

 In an evaluation of Chinook salmon diets collected from mainstem and off-channel habitats (rkm
 7–231), Goertler et al. (2016b) found that planktonic prey were more common in the diets of fish
 captured in the mainstem. Insects were more prevalent in the diets of fish from off-channel
 habitats, although there was no evidence that estimated consumption rates were different
 between the two habitat types (Goertler et al. 2016b). Despite being larger, more genetically
 diverse, and having a greater proportion of hatchery fish, subyearling Chinook salmon sampled
 from deepwater mainstem habitats ate similar prey items compared to subyearling Chinook
 salmon captured in tidal wetland channels (rkm 43–105). Chironomids occurred in more than

Synthesis of the Literature 40

 80% of the diets of fish in both habitat types, although amphipods were more numerically
 dominant in the diets of fish from deepwater mainstem habitats (Sather et al. 2020b).

 Fish Diet and the Role of Microhabitats

 Evaluating microhabitats helps to understand how juvenile salmonids interact with the physical
 environment. These patterns and conditions provide opportunities to develop considerations
 relevant to restoration planning and design. For example, large distributary wetland channels
 and shoreline flats may create favorable conditions for the production of amphipods, compared
 to small dendritic channel networks that may favor production of terrestrial insects (Spilseth and
 Simenstad 2011). Research has shown that juvenile Chinook salmon and steelhead foraging in
 the CRE has commonly occurred in the mid-water column or at the water surface, as evidenced
 by a notable proportion of pupal and adult insects in salmon diets (Daly et al. 2014; Spilseth and
 Simenstad 2011; Lott 2004). However, it is worth noting that many of these prey items originate
 from the benthos, meaning that activities that influence or impact benthic environments are
 likely to affect salmon foraging opportunities throughout the water column.

 Information on prey resource partitioning by salmon of different sizes has generally suggested
 few differences exist, although there is evidence that diets of larger salmon differ from smaller
 fish. Accola et al. (2025) found no differences in diets of fish captured in wetlands across three
 fork length bins of Chinook salmon < 100 mm FL. Similarly, using five fork length bins for
 Chinook salmon, including one that accounted for fish >100 mm FL, Spilseth and Simenstad
 (2011) reported there were no differences in diets of fish across the various size classes.
 Interestingly, this study found that while the diets of Chinook salmon were not different across
 different-sized fish, there were differences in diets of threespine stickleback: Smaller stickleback
 consumed more zooplankton and larger stickleback consumed more amphipods and
 oligochaetes. In a study of Chinook salmon diets in wetland channels (rkm 43–105), fish size
 was a strong predictor of the biomass of individual prey in the diet; larger fish consumed more
 amphipods and adult insects while smaller fish consumed higher proportions of the smaller
 zooplankton (Sather et al. 2026). Lott (2004) noted a similar pattern in wetland habitats (rkm
 35–42) where amphipods and larger insects were more prevalent in the diets of larger Chinook
 salmon. Larger juvenile salmon have also been reported to have more variation within their diets
 (Spilseth and Simenstad 2011), likely because they can exploit a broader range of microhabitats
 and conditions, and can overcome gape limitation, compared to smaller fish.

 Studies examining invertebrate productivity from multiple microhabitats (e.g. benthos, water
 column, water surface) with parallel comparisons to salmon diets have shown that fish exploit all
 available habitats when capturing prey (Spilseth and Simenstad 2011; Storch and Sather 2011;
 Lott 2004). Differences in resource partitioning across different microhabitats, where Chinook
 salmon have shown evidence of increased foraging from the mid-water and surface habitats,
 compared to foraging in the benthos by threespine stickleback, helps to ameliorate competition
 for prey resources (Spilseth and Simenstad 2011). Coupled with the high productivity of
 invertebrates in the tidal wetland channels, Spilseth and Simenstad (2011) concluded there was
 little evidence to suggest competition for prey resources.

 In a comparison of Chinook salmon diets between floodplain habitats that were dominated by
 different vegetative cover (invasive reed canary grass and natural emergent wetlands),
 Klopfenstein (2016) noted subtle differences in fish diets. Fish sampled from reed canary grass
 wetlands consumed more copepods and chironomid larvae compared to the fish diets sampled
 from the natural marshes. Regardless, there was notable overlap in the diets of fish sampled
 from wetland habitats of differing vegetative cover (Klopfenstein 2016). Using parasites found in

Synthesis of the Literature 41

 subyearling Chinook salmon as an indicator for habitat associations and foraging conditions,
 Claxton et al. (2013) found that juvenile salmon foraging conditions could be distinguished
 between sandy, nearshore sites, emergent wetlands, and scrub-shrub wetlands.

 3.3.7 How Do Foraging Conditions Affect Fish Condition and Growth in the
 CRE?

 3.3.7.1 Salmon Stomach Fullness

 Salmon diet investigations not only help to provide insights into what types of prey are being
 eaten and foraging conditions across a range of habitats, they also help to inform aspects
 related to fish condition. Typically, less than 2% of fish sampled for gut content analysis in the
 CRE have had empty stomachs (Weitkamp et al. 2022; Accola et al. 2025; Goertler et al. 2016b;
 Sather et al. 2026). This provides evidence that juvenile salmon and steelhead are actively
 foraging in the CRE habitats studied: deepwater and nearshore mainstem, off-channels, and
 wetland channels. One exception to this trend was noted by Daly et al. (2014), who found that
 half of juvenile steelhead sampled in the lower estuary (rkm 13–15) had empty stomachs.

 In a landscape-scale investigation of fish migrating in deepwater mainstem habitats from below
 Bonneville Dam (rkm 210) to the lower estuary (rkm 15), steelhead had lower stomach fullness
 compared to yearling Chinook salmon (Weitkamp et al. 2022), yet empty stomachs made up
 less than 2% of the samples. In this study, Weitkamp et al. (2022) found a gradient effect
 associated with stomach fullness for Chinook salmon and steelhead with fullness decreasing
 from upstream to downstream. Regardless, yearling Chinook salmon and steelhead were
 actively feeding during their migration through the CRE (Weitkamp et al. 2022).

 In wetland channels, Accola et al. (2025) found stomach fullness of subyearling Chinook salmon
 was similar at sites from rkm 53–149, and fish from the wetland channel in the lowest site (rkm
 6) had higher stomach fullness compared to fish at the uppermost site (rkm 149), although the
 authors note these trends may have been confounded by small sample sizes at these locations.
 The finding of no differences in stomach fullness across months, coupled with high fullness
 values, helped to support the conclusion that subyearling Chinook salmon from this study were
 not prey limited (Accola et al. 2025).

 3.3.7.2 Fish Growth Measured from Otoliths: Landscape-Scale and Seasonal Patterns

 Otoliths (ear bones), from juvenile Chinook salmon have been used as a tool to evaluate trends
 in growth in the CRE. In a study of more than 20 wetlands from rkm 8–230, Chittaro et al. (2018)
 found that growth rates of Chinook salmon, measured from otolith analyses, decreased from
 upstream to downstream locations in the CRE – a pattern that matched the decrease in
 stomach fullness of yearling Chinook salmon and steelhead in the mainstem observed by
 Weitkamp et al. (2022). In contrast, Goertler et al. (2016b) did not observe any longitudinal
 trends in growth of subyearling Chinook salmon captured in nearshore mainstem and off-
 channel sites from rkm 7–231.

 Processes and mechanisms influencing primary and secondary productivity are closely linked
 with seasons, yet the relationship among temporal factors and salmon growth is less clear.
 Goertler et al. (2016) noted that temporal factors were not strong indicators of fish growth.
 Chittaro et al. (2018) suggested that reductions in growth rates for juvenile Chinook salmon may
 have been linked to a combination of factors attributed to seasonal changes in the CRE, such
 as increased water temperatures that also corresponded with an increase in the abundances of

Synthesis of the Literature 42

 other fish species. The timing of entry into the estuary may be relevant in governing growth
 patterns. For example, Chinook salmon that entered the estuary from winter through early
 spring had lower growth rates compared to fish entering the estuary from mid-spring through
 summer (Campbell 2010).

 3.3.7.3 Fish Growth Measured from a Combination of Techniques: Landscape- and
 Habitat-scale Patterns

 Investigations of fish condition and growth have used a variety of approaches, including
 condition indices, physiological markers, and bioenergetics modeling. Comparisons of fish size,
 growth, and condition across different habitats, have yielded a range of results indicating spatial
 considerations have some or no effects on juvenile salmon and steelhead. For example,
 Chinook salmon encountered in the lower segments of the estuary (rkm 6–13) were larger, yet
 had lower condition than Chinook salmon sampled further upstream (rkm 58–60) (Roegner and
 Teel 2014). While stomach fullness of yearling Chinook salmon and steelhead was found to
 decrease from rkm 210–15, Weitkamp et al. (2022) noted that insulin-like growth factor, a
 physiological indicator of growth, increased as fish moved downstream.

 Differences in habitat conditions such as more uniform temperatures and reliance on mainstem
 channels for navigation routes by yearling Chinook salmon, compared to a greater range of
 thermal conditions in a diversity of shallow water habitats typically experienced by subyearling
 Chinook salmon, have explained differences in growth rates between different life histories of
 salmon migrating through the CRE (Morrice et al. 2020). Using an individual based model
 coupled with a bioenergetics model, Morrice et al. (2020) calculated daily growth rates for
 Chinook salmon migrating in the CRE and found yearling Chinook salmon had slower growth
 rates, and many did not grow, compared to subyearling Chinook salmon migrating during the
 same periods.

 Using a bioenergetics modeling approach, Klopfenstein (2016) found few differences in growth
 of Chinook salmon, primarily subyearlings, between wetland, confluence, and nearshore
 mainstem habitats. However, within wetlands, growth rates of Chinook salmon were highest for
 fish reared in natural marshes, compared to marshes dominated by reed canary grass, despite
 there being substantial overlap in the diets of fish from these two types of vegetated wetlands
 (Klopfenstein 2016). By taking a multivariate approach using physiological indicators of fish
 condition, Hanson et al. (2012) concluded that Chinook salmon, the majority of which were
 hatchery fish, in off-channel habitats showed greater condition and higher foraging rates than
 fish sampled from nearshore mainstem habitats. These results were somewhat confounded,
 however, by the finding that there were no indications for differences in growth potential by fish
 in these two habitats. Through analysis of otoliths, Goertler et al. (2016b) found that while there
 were no differences in relative consumption rates of Chinook salmon in off-channel and
 nearshore mainstem habitats, fish in off-channel habitats had higher growth rates compared to
 fish in mainstem habitats.

 3.3.8 How Do Environmental Conditions Affect Juvenile Salmon and Steelhead
 Life History Characteristics, Foraging, and Growth?

 3.3.8.1 Temporal Variation

 Temporal variation can play a significant role in the expression of early life history patterns and
 characteristics of juvenile salmon and steelhead, and yet different patterns emerge when
 considering the influence of seasonal and interannual variation. Sather et al. (2016) found

Synthesis of the Literature 43

 significant variation in the density of Chinook salmon across seasons, yet densities of fish
 across years were not statistically different (rkm 188–202). Similarly, Chinook salmon size
 differed across seasons (Roegner et al. 2012; Sather et al. 2016), yet the proportions of these
 size classes did not differ across years (Sather et al. 2016). Moreover, the consistency in
 interannual variation of genetic stock groups occurring in the CRE at different locations provides
 an opportunity to evaluate which genetic stocks are likely to benefit from restoration outcomes,
 given landscape-scale considerations (Teel et al. 2014).

 Seasonal variation observed in salmon foraging characteristics is likely due to biological
 constraints such as fish size and gape limitations but could also reflect changes in productivity
 and availability of prey. For example, low productivity in winter may impact fish condition
 (Roegner and Teel 2014), whereas increased productivity during summer months is likely linked
 to an increase in availability of energy dense insects (Accola et al. 2025). Consumption of prey
 resources across microhabitats by juvenile salmon from early spring through summer has been
 linked to changes in abundance and availability of prey items (Spilseth and Simenstad 2011).

 Fish foraging patterns across years have varied. In tidal wetland channels (rkm 43–105), the
 sampling year explained only 6% of the variation in subyearling Chinook salmon diets, indicating
 notable overlap in diets of fish between years (Sather et al. 2026). Similarly, other studies of
 subyearling Chinook salmon in wetland channels have reported remarkable consistency in
 consumption of prey across years (Accola et al. 2025; Haskell and Tiffan 2011). In a study of
 the mainstem (rkm 15–210), Weitkamp et al. (2022) found higher stomach fullness and larger
 yearling Chinook salmon during 2017 compared to 2016; however, juvenile steelhead yielded
 opposite results with stomach fullness lower and fish sizes smaller during 2017. In this study,
 both species consumed more chironomids and amphipods during 2017. These interannual
 differences were attributed to abnormally high river discharge during 2017, which may have
 resulted in increased scouring within the benthos, enhancing availability of prey (Weitkamp et al.
 2022). In the lower estuary mainstem (rkm 15), Norrie et al. (2022) did not detect differences in
 growth rates for yearling Chinook salmon collected during 2016 and 2017 but did find that fish
 condition factor was lower in 2016.

 3.3.8.2 Temperature

 Thermal conditions are an important consideration for the health and condition of juvenile
 salmon in the CRE. Research has shown that shallow water habitats in the CRE regularly
 exceed thermal conditions reported as optimal for juvenile salmon (c.f., EPA 2003). Yet,
 research has also shown that during periods of temperature extremes, salmon occupy and
 forage in shallow water habitats. For example, Accola et al. (2025) noted that diets of
 subyearling Chinook salmon sampled in wetland channels were stable, even during periods of
 sub-optimal temperatures. In off-channel and nearshore mainstem habitats, Chinook salmon
 showed increased growth from spring to fall, including during periods when water temperatures
 exceeded 20 °C (Goertler et al. 2016b). Roegner and Teel (2014) did not find evidence that fish
 condition was adversely affected by increased temperatures, but rather, Chinook salmon
 sampled from shallow water habitats at rkm 58–60 showed evidence for high condition, despite
 suboptimal temperatures.

 While there is not an abundance of evidence for a measured effect to growth, condition, or
 foraging during suboptimal temperatures in the CRE, it is worth noting that densities of Chinook
 salmon decrease considerably during periods of warm temperatures (Accola et al. 2025;
 Roegner and Teel 2014; Sather et al. 2016). Through a bioenergetics modeling approach that
 evaluated cohorts of Chinook salmon growth over a 24-month period, Storch (2011) provided

Synthesis of the Literature 44

 insights into why some of these trends may have been observed. Storch (2011) noted Chinook
 salmon growth rates were generally positive, despite temperatures exceeding 22°C. While the
 models noted negative growth during some suboptimal thermal conditions, this trend was not
 consistently observed. Instead, growth was found to be adversely impacted when extreme
 temperatures were sustained over extended periods – e.g., many days. Moreover, growth
 conversion efficiency, an indication of a fish’s ability to convert food into growth, was influenced
 more by thermal maximums than variation in diet, suggesting temperature has a stronger effect
 on growth than food availability (Storch 2011). High feeding intensities, positive growth, and
 high condition measured by Chinook salmon suggest prey availability is suitable to meet energy
 needs ameliorating effects of suboptimal temperatures in shallow water habitats (Roegner and
       Teel 2014; Accola et al. 2025; Goertler et al. 2016b; Storch 2011).

 3.3.9 What Factors and Conditions Influence Invertebrate Production in the
 CRE?

 The historical loss of wetland habitats in the CRE is hypothesized to have resulted in changes to
 trophic dynamics from an ecosystem reliant on macro detritus to one dominated by planktonic
 production, fueled in part by the reservoirs above Bonneville Dam (Sherwood et al. 1990).
 Although plankton assemblages and the mechanisms that influence these organisms are linked
 to food web dynamics in the CRE (Rollwagen-Bollens et al. 2022), the direct interactions and
 implications for salmon foraging conditions are less clear (Maier et al. in prep). Phytoplankton
 assemblages in shallow wetland channel habitats are different than those in mainstem habitats,
 and production of these assemblages is linked to hydraulic connectivity, flow, and water
 temperature (Tausz 2015). For example, disconnected channels were associated with high
 water temperatures, higher variations in dissolved oxygen levels, and higher levels of
 phytoplankton productivity, compared to the mainstem.

 Tausz (2015) hypothesized that higher primary productivity may translate into an increase in
 macroinvertebrate productivity, perhaps counter balancing subpar water quality conditions for
 juvenile salmon. Another possible connection with salmon food webs can be found with lethal
 parasites such as, chytrid fungi, that infect diatoms. Diatoms are not typically edible to
 zooplankton, a food source for juvenile salmonids. However, the parasites convert large diatoms
 into zoospores, rendering them edible by zooplankton. High abundances of diatoms in CRE
 wetland channels present an opportunity for increased foraging potential by zooplankton
 through parasitic infections (Tausz 2015).

 Breckenridge et al. (2015) found that plankton assemblages varied with river discharge and that
 spatial variation was notable from rkm 0–29. Connelly et al. (2020) also found that zooplankton
 assemblage differed between the estuary mouth and tidal freshwater sites, yet there were few
 differences across the longitudinal gradient of tidal freshwater sites from rkm 64–197.
 Zooplankton abundance peaked during summer when water temperatures were highest and
 river discharge was lowest (Connelly et al. 2020). However, Rollwagen-Bollens et al. (2022)
 found that water nutrient concentrations are lowest during summer months (July–August) and
 concluded these conditions may impact primary productivity and food web dynamics. Juvenile
 salmonids use CRE habitats year-round (Sather et al. 2016; Roegner et al. 2012), yet summer
 months coincide with a dramatic reduction in salmon populations migrating through the CRE.
 This makes it difficult to fully understand the how the mechanisms influencing phytoplankton
 during the summer influence foraging conditions for populations of juvenile salmon. For
 example, Goertler et al. (2016b) noted planktonic prey in the diets of Chinook salmon became
 less prevalent from mid-spring to late summer as fish increased consumption of insects. It is

Synthesis of the Literature 45

 unknown whether this was due to a reduction in planktonic prey, an increase in more
 energetically rich prey, a combination of the two, or other unmeasured factors.

 3.3.9.1 Invertebrate Assemblages in CRE Habitats

 Studies of macroinvertebrate assemblages have occurred from the mouth to the upper reaches
 of the CRE. Most research has focused on characterizing invertebrate assemblages in wetland
 channels. Investigations have interrogated an array of spatial scales (micro- to landscape-
 scale), providing insights into dynamics that influence invertebrate assemblages and juvenile
 salmon foraging conditions in the CRE. For example, Johnson and Simenstad (2015) noted that
 insect species richness in wetland habitats increased from the estuary toward Bonneville Dam,
 a pattern that was also correlated with measured increases in habitat complexity.

 The review of literature on salmon foraging has indicated chironomids are a significant
 contributor to salmon diets (reference diet section). Chironomids are also ubiquitous in CRE
 aquatic environments, comprising notable proportions of assemblages in the benthos, the water
 column, and along vegetation and channel margins (Lott 2004; Ramierz 2008; Eaton 2010;
 Haskell and Tiffan 2011). In nearshore habitats (rkm 188–202), chironomids were prevalent in
 the environment (e.g., the benthos, the water column, and along channel margins) and in
 Chinook salmon diets. Yet, in one study, fish were found to frequently select against these items
 as a prey resource, meaning despite high availability and consumption of chironomids, fish
 exploited other resources (Storch and Sather 2011). Partitioning of prey resources through
 space and time helps mitigate effects of intra- and inter-specific competition (Spilseth and
       Simenstad 2011; Eaton 2010).

 Invertebrate production in the benthos is an important consideration for juvenile salmon foraging
 conditions. While research has indicated a large portion of juvenile salmon and steelhead diets
 include pupal and adult insects, foraged from the mid-water column or at the water surface
 (Daly et al. 2014; Spilseth and Simenstad 2011; Lott 2004), many of these insects originate from
 the benthos. Further, larger fish have shown greater diet variation and consumption of benthic
 orienting prey items such as amphipods (Sather et al. 2026; Spilseth and Simenstad 2011; Lott
 2024). Benthic invertebrates have been collected from emergent marshes, scrub-shrub and
 forested wetlands, and from nearshore mainstem and off-channel habitats, and have included a
 diversity of insects, crustaceans, mites, gastropods, bivalves, annelids, and nematodes (Sather
 et al. 2026; Haskell and Tiffan 2011; Lott 2004). An in-depth investigation of benthic invertebrate
 assemblages in off-channel habitats (rkm 129–148) found that items most common in salmon
 diets – chironomids, insects, corophiids, and other crustaceans – accounted for 20–64% of
 benthic invertebrate abundance (Sather et al. 2023).

 Several research efforts have evaluated the transport of invertebrates from wetland channels in
 an effort to understand benefits to juvenile salmon populations in nearby habitats (Ramirez
 2008; Eaton 2010; Roegner and Johnson 2023). In a comparison of invertebrates exported from
 an emergent marsh (rkm 35) and two scrub-shrub wetlands (rkm 77 and 101), Ramirez (2008)
 found the quantity of invertebrates exported at the emergent mash was much lower compared
 to the two scrub-shrub wetlands and surmised that factors such as channel morphology and
 vegetation assemblages in the emergent marsh were more efficient at retaining invertebrates
 compared to conditions at the scrub-shrub wetlands. Ramirez (2008) found no significant
 differences in invertebrate emergence rates between an emergent marsh at Russian Island (rkm
 35), and two scrub/shrub wetlands (rkm 77 and 101) that were characterized by dense reed
 canary grass along the channel margins. In a comparison of wetlands dominated by natural

Synthesis of the Literature 46

 vegetation and reed canary grass (rkm 161), the total density of invertebrates between the two
 marsh types was similar (Klopfenstein 2016).

 Regardless of the differences, both retention and export of invertebrates are important functions
 that influence foraging dynamics for juvenile salmon and steelhead in different ways. Enhanced
 retention may provide increased opportunity for on-site foraging conditions by fish, whereas high
 export of invertebrates from wetland channels provides foraging opportunities for fish in nearby
 distributary channels or mainstem habitats. For example, in the Grays River, during high tide,
 invertebrates in the water column were more abundant upstream of wetlands compared to
 downstream of wetlands. Coupled with the findings that wetlands were exporting more
 invertebrates than were imported, Eaton (2010) demonstrated the role of tidal wetlands in
 providing offsite foraging benefits for juvenile salmon. Roegner and Johnson (2023)
 characterized aspects of invertebrate assemblages exported from emergent marshes and a
 forested wetland and found that forested sites had higher diversity than emergent marshes, yet
 abundances were lower. The energy exported from wetlands was best explained by
 hydrodynamics as well as variation in the composition of invertebrate assemblage (Roegner and
       Johnson 2023).

 Fallout traps have been commonly used to evaluate the assemblage of insects (aquatic and
 terrestrial) and other invertebrates (e.g., spiders, Collembola) associated with wetland channel
 margins or within discrete vegetated patches in marshes. Densities of invertebrates collected
 from fallout traps generally increase through spring and summer months (Spilseth and
 Simenstad 2011). For example, hemipteran density has been shown to increase during summer
 months, which has also corresponded to an increase in these invertebrates in the diets of
 juvenile Chinook salmon (Storch and Sather 2011; Lott 2004). In a comparison of emergent
 marshes to scrub-shrub and forested wetlands, Lott (2004) found that emergent marshes had
 the lowest species diversity in early spring, but by June there were no longer differences in
 invertebrate densities from fallout traps. There were, however, fewer chironomids at forested
 and scrub-shrub wetlands compared to emergent marshes (Lott 2004).

 In what has likely been the most detailed analysis of microhabitats and the effects of taxonomic
 resolution in the CRE, Ramirez (2008) evaluated insect assemblages in wetland channels in
 different elevation gradients ranging from the channel bottom to the high marsh. Many insect
 taxa were present in emergence traps from all microhabitats in an emergent marsh (rkm 35)
 and, when evaluated at the family level, there were no differences in the abundance of
 Chironomidae between the three microhabitats. However, when analyzed to the genera level, it
 was clear that the composition of invertebrates did differ between the microhabitats (Ramirez
 2008). Given the ubiquitousness of chironomids, this finding is worth considering for future study
 designs. Within the emergent marsh, richness of Chironomidae taxa was highest in the wetland
 channels, whereas richness for other insects was highest in the high marsh. Ramirez (2008)
 compared the results of invertebrates collected from the emergent marsh to those collected
 from two scrub-shrub wetlands (rkm 77 and 101) characterized by dense stands of fringing reed
 canary grass. The scrub-shrub wetlands exported higher densities (measured in the neuston)
 and higher diversity (measured from the emergence traps) of invertebrates compared to the
 emergent marsh (Ramirez 2008).

 The coupling of sampling methodologies for evaluating invertebrate characteristics provides a
 more holistic picture of environmental and biological dynamics that influence foraging
 opportunity for juvenile salmonids. For example, Ramirez (2008) demonstrated that the number
 of invertebrates exported in the water column was a fraction of the number of invertebrates
 emerging from the marsh, and while there was overlap in taxa, the sampling methods resulted

Synthesis of the Literature 47

 in assemblage differences – e.g., neuston samples accounted for terrestrial contributions that
 were not found in the emergence assemblages. Comparisons of invertebrate assemblages from
 emergence and fallout traps suggest insects collected from fallout traps provided a good
 indication of prey availability to juvenile salmonids, while emergence traps offered more
 granularity for considering questions about invertebrate microhabitats (Ramirez 2008). Using
 multiple lines of evidence provides a useful approach when trying to observe the signal through
 inherently noisy datasets, particularly as this applies to understanding how conditions at
 restoration sites are responding (Diefenderfer et al. 2011b, 2016). Multiple sampling strategies
 and response variables concerning invertebrate assemblages have helped to demonstrate that
 habitat restoration through hydrologic reconnection can create foraging opportunities for salmon
 (Sather et al. 2026; Haskell and Tiffan 2011).

 3.3.9.2 Invertebrate Assemblages Associated with Native and Non-native Marsh
 Vegetation

 Hypotheses concerning the production of invertebrates among native and non-native vegetation
 have been tested by two separate investigations. In a comparison of a native vegetated marsh
 with a wetland dominated by reed canary grass (rkm 161), Klopfenstein (2016) used fallout and
 emergence traps to understand community assemblage characteristics of invertebrates.
 Chironomids and other Diptera were the most prevalent invertebrate groups sampled from
 emergence traps in a native vegetated marsh and a reed canary grass dominated wetland.
 Total density of emergent invertebrates was highest in the reed canary grass wetland, yet total
 density of invertebrates collected from fallout traps was highest in natural marshes. A
 multivariate comparison of abundance and diversity of invertebrates indicated no differences
 between the two vegetation types, although there were some differences observed from month
 to month (Klopfenstein 2016). The reed canary grass wetland had a greater abundance of
 Collembola and more Hymenoptera (Klopfenstein 2016), both of which are found in juvenile
 salmon diets (Eaton 2010; Sather et al. 2026; Storch and Sather 2011).

 To evaluate detritus and macroinvertebrates characteristics in patches of native and non-native
 vegetation, Cordell et al. (2023) used multiple response variables to test differences between
 reed canary grass and Lyngbye’s sedge patches in wetlands from rkm 50 and 89. Using data
 collected from fallout traps and emergence traps, the analysis found no differences in the total
 invertebrate average density or biomass between the two types of vegetation. There were no
 significant differences in the invertebrate assemblages from the fallout and emergence trap
 data, nor were there assemblage differences in the benthos between the vegetation types
       (Cordell et al. 2023).

 While many of the response variables used in the Cordell et al. (2023) study indicated no
 differences between the vegetation types, there was some evidence to suggest different taxa
 within the assemblages may respond differently to temporal and spatial factors. For example,
 abundances and biomass of chironomids and Diptera were higher in fallout traps and
 emergence traps within Lyngbye’s sedge, and similar to the findings of Klopfenstein (2016),
 Collembola were predominant in reed canary grass. Invertebrate diversity from fallout and
 emergence traps was higher in reed canary grass during April and May, and by June diversity
 was similar between both vegetation types. Differences in invertebrate characteristics between
 vegetation types was clearer from the benthic data, where total average invertebrate density, as
 well as the densities of dipterans, and chironomids were higher in Lyngbye’s sedge patches, yet
 total average invertebrate biomass was not statistically different between the two vegetation
 types. Removal of annelids and nematodes from the biomass analysis resulted in a higher

Synthesis of the Literature 48

 biomass of chironomids in Lyngbye’s sedge compared to reed canary grass (Cordell et al.
 2023).

 3.4 Summary of Literature Review Findings
 CEERP restoration and research activities have benefited from the historical context of prior
 research, which in many cases helped to improve our understanding of knowledge gaps and
 streamline identification of research questions. While research prior to 2000 provided insightful
 findings on the environmental and biological conditions in the estuary, we noted that the majority
 of these studies were limited in their spatial extent to the lower 100 km of the estuary. While the
 early stages of CEERP research likewise focused efforts in the lowest segments of the estuary
 and primarily in tidal wetlands, as CEERP developed, interest in understanding the extent of
 habitat use by juvenile salmon led to an increase in research across a broader range of habitats
 and time-scales.

 The results of our literature review and synthesis of key findings provide evidence that research
 has produced major advances in understanding of the CRE during the past 25 years. The
 overall status of the CRE system remains heavily modified and managed. Non-native fish,
 plants, and invertebrates are well established in shallow water habitats throughout the CRE.
 The effects of these non-native species on the ecosystem, on restoration outcomes, and on
 juvenile salmonids are not fully understood. Yet, the extensive research implemented under
 CEERP has shown there are diverse salmon and steelhead early life histories using CRE
 habitats across a range of habitats and time periods.

 Our review confirmed some research findings prior to CEERP. For instance, it was known that
 insects and amphipods were an important foraging base for juvenile salmon, and that the
 relative size classes of juvenile salmon using deep water were larger than fish found in shallow
 water habitat. Our review also uncovered unpublished historical research and mapping reports
 treating many aspects of the wetland ecosystem that appear to have been overlooked during
 the early planning phases of CEERP, which would have helped to plan research in tidal river
 reaches mistakenly thought to have been previously excluded.

 The following sections provide an overview of key findings detailed in Sections 3.1 through 3.3.

 3.4.1 Physical Processes

 With respect to physical processes, understanding the spatially and temporally complex CRE
 has been the work of decades. The current system faces considerable uncertainty with respect
 to future trends and stressors, but tools and datasets are available to assess both system- and
 wetland-scale impacts. Advances in modeling, computing, and both in situ (e.g., CMOP) and
 remote (e.g., satellite, drone, LiDAR) observations have filled many knowledge gaps at the
 system-scale. This progress includes the numerical hydrodynamic models—Delft3D-FM, AdH,
 Schism, FVCOM—as well as analytical tools to quantify trends such as sea level rise, tidal
 characteristics, and water temperature.

 Due to flow regulation and irrigation, the magnitude of the freshet has decreased, and annual
 maxima have shifted earlier. Further shifts due to climate change are ongoing and include
 increasing winter flows and decreasing late summer flows. We also note that climate-driven
 shifts will be modified by any changes to Bonneville operations, including the forthcoming
 Columbia River Treaty.

Synthesis of the Literature 49

 Water levels are driven by tides from rkm 0–87 and river flow from rkm 150–234, and by a
 combination of these controlling factors from rkm 87–150. Mainstem deepening has led to
 decrease in MWL in the CRE at mean to low flows. Floodplain levees have constricted flow and
 increased water velocity and bed stress. Increasing coastal tidal range and engineering
 modifications have increased the tidal range in the CRE. Current relative sea-level rise
 estimates are 0.4–2.8 mm/yr at Astoria, but these estimates are being supplemented by
 modeling efforts across the CRE. Coseismic subsidence may occur with the next Cascadia
 earthquake and result in sudden water level and plant community shifts in large areas.

 Prior to development of onset of logging, levee construction, in-channel wood removal, and
 other channel engineering in the 1840s, floodplains kept pace with water level changes for
 thousands of years. However, modern sediment supply to the CRE is a fraction of what is once
 was due to dam impoundment and flow regulation. Advances in hydrodynamic modeling, global
 climate modeling, and accretion measurement techniques will allow us to evaluate whether
 wetland accretion is sufficient to compensate for projected SLR and retain wetland vegetation
 types.

 3.4.2 Wetland and Other Shallow-Water Habitats

 Prior to 2000, much was known about wetland vegetation, its controlling factors and spatial
 patterns, and historical change. Since then, through the CEERP program, the work of federal
 agency and university researchers has continuously built on prior and emerging knowledge and
 methods. By the early 1970s, the general pattern of plant associations and frequency of
 inundation along the elevation gradient from channels had been shown to progress from tidal
 flats to low and high marsh, willows, and forested wetlands and those communities had been
 described with species lists from the river mouth to Bonneville. It had been observed that these
 reflected a great deal of disturbance in the herb layer (Christy and Putera 1993). The upriver
 extent of salinity-tolerant plants was known and had been accounted for in a habitat zonation to
 rkm 64.

 Despite the baseline knowledge available in 2000, relationships between hydrology, elevation,
 and vegetation were only generally understood. They had not been established through data
 collection with sufficient spatiotemporal resolution to provide predictive information to restoration
 planners and engineers. Habitats upriver of rkm 64 were studied intensively by CEERP using
 modern real-time kinematic survey methods and datalogging technology for water surface
 elevation and extending farther up tributaries than earlier studies, producing ecohydrological
 models useful to restoration planners and engineers. Yet the possibility of revisiting 1970s study
 sites on the nearshore mainstem and nearby wetlands was not raised because the prior work by
 Tabor (1976a, b) had not been incorporated in the foundations of CEERP (NMFS 2000).

 Several schema for plant classifications had been proposed before CEERP, utilizing the data,
 methods, and understanding of their times and the perspectives and aims of the respective
 researchers treating different geographic scopes. Through CEERP, historical and 2009 land
 cover datasets have been merged in a single spatial database for restoration planning and
 prioritization purposes within the program (Cannon 2015; Ramirez et al. 2019). While invasive
 plant species had been recognized prior to CEERP, the unique challenges of controlling them in
 tidal freshwater systems have been advanced under CEERP. We now know that a majority of
 wetland plots sampled have reed canary grass (Borde et al. 2020); and that control methods will
 be more successful if they are begun prior to other restoration actions including hydrologic
 reconnection (Kidd 2017; Sinks et al. 2021; Borde et al. in revision).

Synthesis of the Literature 50

 Published analysis of habitat data collected on restoration sites together with their associated
 reference sites is insufficient to determine the trajectories of wetland restoration or, in the
 absence of performance targets, “success.” The ERTG has revisited selected sites to observe
 outcomes, and for this purpose, sponsors prepared dossiers of monitoring data (ERTG 2025a).
 Yet it is known that some post-restoration metrics lag, in particular plant community
 development, which may be impacted by invasive species, grazing, or other hypothetical as-yet-
 untested factors such as the appropriate timing and distribution of propagules from native plants
 (Kidd 2017; Sinks et al. 2021; Borde et al. in revision).

 3.4.3 Salmon Habitat Use and Invertebrate Assemblages

 Historical research along with studies from the early stages of CEERP focused on habitats
 below rkm 100. When research on salmon in tidal freshwater habitats began in 2006, the extent
 to which the habitats above rkm 100 were relevant to juvenile salmonids was largely unknown.
 Moreover, the potential benefits provided by the CRE were widely perceived as not relevant to
 upriver salmon and steelhead stock groups due to the fast migration rates of these fish. In the
 last 25 years, CEERP research has provided evidence that juvenile salmon and steelhead
 benefit from an array of habitats from Bonneville Dam to the mouth. Subyearling Chinook
 salmon are most frequently encountered in shallow water habitats in the CRE, and these fish
 demonstrate a notable breadth of life history diversity. The extensive research on genetic stock
 distribution of Chinook salmon in the CRE has led to an understanding that multiple stock
 groups occur in the system at the same time and salmon in the CRE have originated from
 above and below Bonneville dam. Juvenile salmon migrate into the CRE throughout the year,
 and some species have demonstrated extended residency in shallow water habitats. While
 juvenile salmon have been the central focus of fishery research in the CRE, they typically
 compose less than 5% of the fish assemblage, providing evidence that a wider lens may be
 required to interpret research findings, especially in the context of ecosystem and habitat
 functions.

 Research on salmon foraging and invertebrate assemblages has provided key insights on
 functional conditions offered by a wide range of habitats in the CRE. There is a notable amount
 of overlap in the diets of juvenile salmon of different sizes and genetic stocks of origin, and
 among different habitat types. There has not been evidence of differences in growth rates for
 different genetic stocks of Chinook salmon, nor has there been substantial evidence to suggest
 that juvenile salmon foraging conditions are markedly distinct among different habitat types.
 Juvenile salmon are opportunistic feeders and consume many of the same prey items across
 different spatial scales in the CRE. One study found that Chinook salmon and steelhead in
 deepwater mainstem habitats, many of which originated from above Bonneville dam, ate similar
 prey items to juvenile salmonids captured in tidal wetland channels. These findings suggest that
 connectivity of habitats in the CRE provided off-site benefits for fish that may not otherwise
 directly access shallow water habitats.

 Invertebrate data, much like other sources of biological data, can be quite variable. While
 numerous studies have examined differences in invertebrate communities across different
 spatial scales, it is difficult to overcome naturally high variation in environmental and biological
 factors while also contending with challenges such as sample size, lack of replication, and
 unbalanced study designs. These challenges are not unique to studies of invertebrates, yet the
 tendency to deploy numerous analytical approaches to many response variables can result in
 many outcomes, some of which can be confounding.

Synthesis of the Literature 51

 3.4.4 Key Integrative Advances in Understanding

 Advances in the three subject areas discussed above are important in isolation, but salmon
 recovery relies on understanding interactions between trends in environmental drivers,
 environmental conditions, and biological responses. Certain research since the inception of
 CEERP has successfully integrated data and information from across the salmon, physical
 process, and/or wetland ecosystem subject areas framed in this review to advance new
 understanding, and in some cases new models.

 3.4.4.1 Spatiotemporal Complexity

 We found it useful to conceptualize the CRE as a continuum from the river mouth to Bonneville
 dam, with gradients of environmental conditions in both the lateral and along-channel directions,
 up tributaries, and onto the floodplain (similar to Bunn and Arthington 2002). Importantly, the
 body of reviewed research has indicated that there is no “one size fits all” gradient or continuum
 that may be applied heuristically across all three subject areas reviewed here. Many studies
 have conflicting results with regard to site scale and landscape scale variation. This is
 unsurprising because of the complexity of biological responses and the high environmental
 variability. It is clear from this review that the spatiotemporal gradients differ for response
 variables relevant to juvenile salmon, invertebrates, plants, hydrology, channels, and sediments.
 This is crucial for both future research studies examining multiple factors and for restoration
 planning, design, and engineering.

 The responses of juvenile salmon, invertebrates, plants, hydrology, channels, and sediments
 aggregate many, multivariate influences at different spatial and temporal scales. This
 emphasizes the importance of the spatiotemporal scale(s) of observation in study designs, and
 poses limitations on the extrapolation of results broadly across the CRE. Moreover, the
 spatiotemporal dynamics across this multivariate system are changing. In particular, the current
 dynamic continuum is shifting due to long-term climate change, meaning previously collected
 data are less representative of future trends, affecting the uncertainty bounds of predictive
 modeling.

 3.4.4.2 Habitat Types and Conditions

 There has been research into different habitat types and how fish and invertebrates have
 experienced or responded to different habitat conditions. Through all of that work, the key
 finding has been that all habitats play important roles in the CRE ecosystem. The early phases
 of CEERP focused on salmon habitat associations in wetland channels, largely below rkm 100.
 Research expanded to areas above 100 rkm to include investigations of off-channel and
 floodplain habitat use as well as use of the mainstem by juvenile salmon. Regardless of habitat
 type, juvenile salmon and steelhead access a diversity of habitats that create foraging and
 growth opportunities for fish.

 Water temperature has been one of the most widely measured variables under CEERP.
 Shallow water habitats in the CRE can exceed levels recommended for juvenile salmonids. Yet,
 research has shown that during periods of warm temperatures, fish continue to forage and grow
 and condition are generally favorable. Bioenergetics modeling results have indicated fish were
 adversely impacted by temperature when thermal extremes occur over a period of days. We
 acknowledge that research teams are currently finalizing analyses of water temperature at
 various spatio-temporal scales and expect advancements in the implications for restoration and
 salmonid habitat suitability in the near future.

Synthesis of the Literature 52

 Water surface elevation dynamics together with floodplain elevations are a controlling factor in
 wetland plant communities and access for juvenile salmonids into habitats. Tidal wetland water
 surface elevation dynamics have been quantitatively linked to mainstem water levels across the
 tidal river and upper estuary. This has supported modeling of floodplain conditions using data
 collected from the mainstem, an approach that may offer efficiencies in future monitoring efforts.
 The spatial distribution of these gradients along the CRE continuum is now far better
 understood.

 3.4.4.3 Long-term Ecosystem Change

 Since the 1980s, the historical record has been used to inform salmon habitat restoration in the
 PNW including the impacts on salmonids of 20th-century riparian land and water management
 practices. In early years of the CEERP program, the loss of shallow-water habitat area was
 attributed to the hydropower system. However, advances in data, analytical techniques, and a
 broader geographic scope throughout the CRE have shown that diking was the major historical
 cause of shallow-water habitat area reduction. This analysis confirmed that the dike breaching
 based hydrologic reconnection restoration methods central to CEERP are an important
 approach to helping to rectify historical habitat loss for juvenile salmon. The ecohydrology
 models developed to predict elevation changes after the next subduction zone earthquake
 further allow risk planning to be built into restoration project prioritization and assessment of
 habitat change.

 The dominant historical extent of tidal forest and scrub-shrub wetlands relative to emergent
 marshes in the 1800s has only been recognized in the scientific literature during the CEERP
 period and affects the plant community aims of ecosystem restoration. This finding also has
 encouraged research leading to concurrent increases in understanding of the unique
 importance of microtopography and large wood for salmon habitat in the tidal and other low-
 elevation wetlands of the Pacific Northwest. This report further revealed the existence of
 intermediate data points such as species lists for aquatic and terrestrial flora and fauna that
 were produced for the CRE riparian zone decades prior to CEERP, but not included in the 2000
 Biological Opinion or in founding documents of CEERP. These aerial photo interpretations and
 field surveys from the 1970s are now available online and help to clarify historical ecosystem
 trajectories such as the prevalence of reed canary grass at the time as discussed herein.

Synthesis of the Literature 53

 4.0 Cumulative Restoration Actions and Effectiveness
 Monitoring of CEERP Habitat Benefits for Juvenile
 Salmon
 Beginning in 2014, there was a concerted effort to establish annual restoration and monitoring
 plans (BPA/Corps 2014); the most recent was BPA/Corps (2026). Annual tracking streamlines
 reporting and information sharing and has added value to summarizing and disseminating
 information on progress (Figure 6). Our review identified 27 peer reviewed papers on CRE
 restoration published between 2008-2026, 10 of which did not include data collected from
 restoration sites.

 Figure 6. The cumulative restoration of the CRE floodplain, 2004–2025, not including
 acquisition. (Adapted by A. Whiting, BPA, 2026; from Littles et al. 2022.) Figure
 Courtesy of A. Whiting, BPA.

 Data from 17 CEERP restoration sites are included in the published papers identified in this
 review. Between 2012−2025, more than 60 restoration sites have been monitored (Table 4,
 Table 5), with data collection occurring across multiple years. While there has been an
 appreciable site-scale effort to collect monitored indicators such as water surface elevation,

Cumulative Restoration Actions and Effectiveness Monitoring 54

 temperature, and sediment accretion data at restoration sites, a subset of the CEERP
 restoration projects have comprehensively evaluated restoration effectiveness using fish-
 centered response variables (Bottom et al. 2025; Table 5). Despite the limited number of sites
 evaluated, it is clear that salmon access and forage in re-connected habitats. Some of the first
 action effectiveness research and monitoring efforts were associated with the Grays River, a
 tributary to the CRE (rkm 34). The removal of a tide gate in the Grays followed by installation of
 large twin culverts provided access for fish, including subyearling Chinook salmon, created new
 foraging opportunities for juvenile salmon, and increased fish species diversity (Roegner et al.
 2010). Tidal-hydrological dynamics quickly equilibrated to the channel system outside the
 breached dike (Diefenderfer et al. 2008). Post-restoration site succession includes the addition
 of logs from trees naturally felled by flooding channels, which can provide habitat for juvenile
 salmon analogous to an adjacent reference site (Diefenderfer and Montgomery 2009).

 The record of action effectiveness monitoring through 2025 is given in Table 4 (site-years and
 monitoring leads) and Table 5 (monitoring metrics).
