Cumulative Restoration Actions and Effectiveness Monitoring 62

 Craig et al. (2014) evaluated restoring marshes in the Grays River and found subyearling coho
 salmon that occupied newly reconnected emergent marshes had higher growth rates compared
 to forested wetlands and mainstem habitats. Culverts also present barriers to fish migration and
 access to habitats. Following a culvert modification project to improve fish passage, juvenile
 salmon were collected from a flood plain area at Mirror Lake (rkm 208), indicating fish passed
 through the newly modified project. However, there wasn’t evidence of an effect on salmonid
 assemblages when comparing before and after conditions (Sol et al. 2019a).

 Monitoring of hydrologic reconnections through dike removal and wetland channel excavations
 has indicated that some indicators at new habitats are often comparable to reference conditions.
 Diet contents and foraging rates of subyearling Chinook salmon at the Crims Island restoration
 site (rkm 87) were similar to those at the reference location (Haskell and Tiffan 2011); larval and
 pupal dipterans were a primary food source for fish at both site types, but fish diets also
 included cladocerans, amphipods, and insects. Median residence time of Chinook salmon at the
 restoration site increased from ~12 h before restoration to ~50 h after restoration. However,
 robust comparisons of pre- and post-restoration residence times were challenged by low sample
 sizes and inconsistencies in sampling efforts (Haskell and Tiffan 2011).

 In a comparison of four restoration-reference site pairs from rkm 43–105, Sather et al. (2026)
 found that while there were some differences among response variables between site type
 (restoration-reference), this factor did not explain large sources of variation in fish diet. There
 was evidence for slightly higher densities of Chinook salmon at restoration sites compared to
 reference sites. However, there were no differences in the condition of subyearling Chinook
 salmon, nor were there differences in genetic stock composition between the four pairs (Sather
 et al. 2020a). McNatt et al. (2020) documented the residence of multiple species of juvenile
 salmon and steelhead in restored and reference tidal channels (rkm 54 and 56) and
 demonstrated that fish from interior Columbia Basin stock groups accessed restoration sites in
 the CRE.

 Foraging characteristics provide additional insights into how fish are using habitats and whether
 conditions at restoring habitats are similar or different from reference sites. For example, fish
 from restoration sites consumed a higher proportion of zooplankton and Collembola while fish
 from reference sites consumed a higher proportion of adult insects, yet there were many
 similarities between diet items consumed by fish and the assemblages of invertebrates sampled
 from microhabitats at the restoration and reference sites (Sather et al. 2026).

 Studying invertebrate assemblages of restoration sites provides an opportunity to understand
 how restoration contributes to foraging opportunities for juvenile salmon and steelhead. Wetland
 restoration has been shown to produce invertebrate assemblages that were similar to those
 sampled from reference sites, yet similar to the results of Chinook salmon diets, site type
 (restoration-reference) was not a significant source of variation in invertebrate assemblages
 (Sather et al. 2026). Interestingly, the use of multiple response variables can sometimes yield
 conflicting conclusions about the outcomes of restoration projects. For example, Haskell and
 Tiffan (2011) noted few changes in the abundance of chironomids between pre- and post-
 restoration conditions, yet this study measured a reduction in total invertebrate diversity in the
 benthos and the water column when comparing conditions before and after restoration.
 Following restoration, Sather et al. (2026) found that abundances of invertebrates sampled from
 the benthos and the water surface were higher in reference sites compared to restoration sites.
 However, abundances of invertebrates sampled in the water column were greater in restoration
 sites compared to reference sites. These findings provide insights into study design

Cumulative Restoration Actions and Effectiveness Monitoring 63

 considerations for research on invertebrate assemblages in the CRE, particularly those seeking
 to understand the influence of spatial scales.

 The USACE is currently considering the use of dredged material for habitat creation projects in
 the CRE. While published findings on the efficacy of dredged material implemented under
 CEERP are limited, research is underway. The Woodland Islands dredged material project,
 constructed in 2020, was the first habitat creation project developed in association with CEERP.
 A before-after-control-impact (BACI) study was implemented to evaluate the response of
 benthic invertebrates to dredged material placement. This study demonstrated that off-channel
 habitats produced diverse benthic invertebrate assemblages, and many were common prey
 items for juvenile salmon. The study also found that invertebrate abundance at the dredged
 material placement site was lower two years after placement as compared to the control sites
 (Sather et al. 2023). Given the limited spatio-temporal scope of this study, additional
 understanding associated with the outcomes at this site and others is needed. For example, the
 extent to which dredge material placement is detrimental or beneficial to juvenile salmon will be
 important in the evaluation of projects (ERTG 2022b).

 The results of action effectiveness research indicate that biological response to restoration is
 complex. While hypothesis testing should be a central element used to frame research on the
 response of habitat restoration, shifting the analytical focus from p-values toward variation
 partitioning provides a more nuanced understanding of ecological processes. For example, an
 analysis of 33 sites, including created, recently breached, and historically breached, found that
 fish communities were more sensitive to spatiotemporal factors than site history (Diefenderfer et
 al. 2024). Understanding major drivers in variability helps inform study design, analysis plans for
 selected response variables, and ultimately the engineering plans and outcomes of restoration
 projects.

Cumulative Restoration Actions and Effectiveness Monitoring 64

 5.0 Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended
 Actions
 This section synthesizes recently published reviews of CEERP by independent scientific panels
 with scientific uncertainties and programmatic needs as informed by the literature review, and
 provides recommended actions. It is worth noting, these recommendations are not designed to
 outline needs at an individual project level. Instead, the framing for this section is intended to be
 generalizable to a range of habitat restoration actions and associated monitoring and research.
 Addressing uncertainties and executing recommended actions will require collaboration among
 the many CEERP participants (Figure 7).

 Figure 7. Illustration of the expertise required to successfully implement CEERP, address
 scientific uncertainties and programmatic needs.

 5.1 Review of CEERP Program Feedback and Recommendations
 Since 2018
 The following section synthesizes previously published CEERP program feedback from three
 major reviews of CEERP and assesses the status of recommendations.

 5.1.1 Independent Peer Review Committee Recommendations

 We synthesized more than three dozen recommendations across the three sources – ERTG
 (2022a), ISRP (2022), and SM2 (Johnson et al. 2018), identifying duplication, overlap,
 complementary ideas, and gaps relative to the SM3 literature review. Recommendations were
 categorized into one of two overarching areas: (1) future research and (2) program planning and
 evaluation. This resulted in eight recommendations directed toward future research
 considerations (Table 6) and 11 recommendations relevant to program planning and evaluation
 (Table 7). Of the eight recommendations for future research made between 2018–2022 (Table
 6), six are underway in whole or in part, and at minimum have been occasionally implemented.
 For instance, climate-change impacts are being modeled under CEERP research and also
 considered in the evaluation of CEERP restoration project proposals. Other studies have
 examined or are actively examining the efficacy of dredged-material placement for habitat
 creation. CEERP research has also modeled and collected in situ data on the drivers of change,

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 65

 and studies on the utility of emerging technologies for monitoring tidal wetlands have been
 implemented.

 Of the 11 recommendations for CEERP relevant to program planning and evaluation made from
 2018–2022 (Table 7), seven have been fully or partially implemented – for example, the
 application of ERTG’s landscape framework conceptual model and improvements to and use of
 Site Evaluation Cards. The cards now include landscape features that are completed by
 restoration practitioners for informative on-site interactions with ERTG members (ERTG 2025a).
 While in some cases there are statistically robust field experiments, improved frequency of
 effectiveness reporting, and clear lines between the application of data to improve future
 restoration and RME, these have not been fully implemented across the program. Commitment
 of long-term funds for RME is underway.

 5.1.2 Synthesis Memo Workshop Research Recommendations

 The 2024 workshop concluded with a synthesis exercise in which the four breakout groups
 brainstormed key CEERP research needs based on their understanding of the state of the
 science as well as gaps and uncertainties. Each group down-selected from their respective lists
 and submitted their top three research questions for review by all workshop participants. The
 resulting 12 questions were individually ranked from most to least important (Box 2).

 Box 2. Top research questions identified by CEERP professionals in 2024, ranked in order of the most
 (1) to fewest (12) votes from workshop participants.

 1. Will the climate and flow related temperature trends restrict habitat availability for juvenile salmon
 thus impacting the utility of restored sites?

 2. How will the interplay between flow changes, SLR, and changes to sediment dynamics affect
 wetland inundation?

 3. What is the current status of the food web in the lower estuary, exotic species and competition?

 4. How does prey quality vary by species, location, season (includes flux)?

 5. Are juvenile salmonids present in restored sites?

 6. What info is needed to design projects resilient to climate change?

 7. How do we expand CEERP to incorporate broader benefits to ecosystem function and services?

 8. What are the key uncertainties with regards to BUDM benefits and tradeoffs?

 9. How are the vegetation and prey communities linked?

 10. Hypothesis: Increase in vegetation diversity from restoration contributes to increase in prey-
 consumption size and weight in juvenile salmon.

 11. Will sediment accretion keep up with sea level rise?

 12. Salmon predation risk and links to habitat restoration.

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 67

 These top research questions (Box 2) illustrate the differences between prioritizing research in
 salmon ecology, physical processes, and wetland ecosystems. We found examples of these
 research questions being studied and believe that with additional focus on application more
 broadly, the program outcomes could be strengthened. We suggest focusing on restoration
 needs to guide research investments. Specifically, what data gaps hamper decision-making
 relative to restoration design, implementation, and long-term outcomes?

 The following two sections address gaps, uncertainties, and recommendations in the scientific
 and programmatic space.

 5.2 Scientific Uncertainties and Needs
 This section synthesizes scientific findings summarized in Section 3.0. In this and the following
 section, general recommendations are translated into implementable actions.

 5.2.1 Adherence to Robust Study Designs and Analytical Approaches

 The CEERP knowledge base has been generated through approaches ranging from collecting
 basic monitored indicators (e.g., water temperature, channel cross sections) to hypothesis
 testing using statistical analyses and modeling techniques. Both ends of the spectrum have
 roles in building knowledge. Yet, we noted that many of the monitoring and research efforts
 could further advance restoration science and improve decisions by adopting hypothesis-driven
 frameworks. There is evidence that not all CEERP monitoring and research efforts are
 accompanied by specific research questions, hypotheses, and robust analytical approaches that
 are considered a priori to data collection efforts. Given the innumerable monitored indicators
 that can be applied in restoration science, it is unclear which have the most value in advancing
 CEERP understanding of restoration outcomes and when success has been achieved.

 • Both monitoring and research activities should be accompanied by clear research
 questions that map to study designs and analysis plans. Connecting these elements
 helps strengthen the outcomes of monitoring and research by maximizing the value of
 limited resources available for field data collection and post processing.
 • When a research or monitoring plan cannot be fully funded, alternatives must be vetted.
 It is likely that the most statistically robust study designs cannot be fully implemented
 because of funding limitations, the practicality of collecting dozens of replicates, and
 other factors. Nevertheless, all studies – including those with a reduced scope to meet
 budget requirements – should have peer-reviewed study designs and analysis plans that
 ensure defensible results are produced and limitations are acknowledged.
 • Future research and monitoring sampling designs, analysis, interpretation, and reporting
 should reflect the locally important environmental conditions and environmental
 gradients known to be relevant to response variables. For example, extrapolating results
 from any given location in the CRE to another location requires quantitative examination
 of all relevant environmental gradients and uncertainty analyses.

 5.2.2 Juvenile Salmon Condition, Growth, and Survival

 Growth and condition of juvenile fish in estuarine habitats is a key indicator of habitat quality.
 There is ample evidence that juvenile salmon in the CRE, including larger migrants typically

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 68

 found in deepwater mainstem habitats, are actively feeding; less than 2% sampled in the CRE
 had empty guts. Yet, there is uncertainty about how growth is related to landscape- and site-
 scale conditions. The interaction between interannual variation and foraging is unknown; some
 studies report similarity in foraging patterns of fish from year to year and others have indicated
 river discharge may affect availability of prey and/or foraging success of salmonids.
 Furthermore, the implications of climate variability for prey productivity, foraging, growth, and
 survival in the CRE are not understood.

 The production of invertebrates, which serve as a prey base for juvenile salmon in the CRE,
 indicates habitat benefits. While the export of prey from wetlands has been linked to off-site
 benefits for salmon in mainstem habitats, the outcomes of related mechanisms are not well
 understood. It remains uncertain whether some wetlands act as net sources, effectively
 transporting prey into adjacent waterbodies, while others retain prey for higher on-site benefits.
 Moreover, most research has not been approached with restoration design and management
 practices in mind. Understanding mechanisms or physical conditions that promote off-site
 versus on-site benefits presents an intriguing opportunity for strategic restoration planning.

 • Develop a white paper to comprehensively evaluate methods for measuring fish
 condition and growth and recommend improvements to studies using growth metrics as
 response variables. A wide range of approaches – e.g., otoliths, biochemical indicators,
 modeling – have been applied in the CRE and other systems. The paper should address
 the advantages and limitations of each as well as the study design elements (e.g.,
 sample size and statistical power, replication, stratification) required for research
 questions and hypotheses.
 • We recommend that research on invertebrate productivity consider how results can
 inform restoration planning and design; i.e., how environmental factors that influence
 production and export promote on-site versus off-site benefits. Research in this space
 should integrate elements of physical processes (e.g., 3D hydrodynamic modeling and
 non-passive particle tracking) and thermal conditions. The research should consider
 which micro-habitats, prey pools, and collection methods (e.g., benthic samples, neuston
 tows, emergence traps, fallout traps) would provide the best insights into mechanisms
 for production, transport, and fate.
 • Life-cycle models are an opportunity to explore how the estuary supports growth and
 survival of juvenile salmon and how restoration may increase survival in salmon
 populations. For example, carry-over effects on fitness from estuary to coastal areas
 have been cited as important considerations, yet workshop participants indicated that
 life-cycle models incorporating the estuary have not been completed. Because the
 estuary would be represented as one component in a larger model, a life-cycle model
 would necessitate partnership with scientists having relevant expertise in the basin,
 tributaries, and ocean. CEERP researchers and managers should consider opportunities
 to collaborate with experts working outside of the CRE to explore data needs related to
 “the estuary box” of such a model. It would be strategic to understand whether RME
 efforts could be tailored to collect data that inform multiple program needs.

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 69

 5.2.3 Predicting Effects of Ecosystem Drivers on Resilience at Estuary, Reach,
 and Restoration Site Scales

 Projections of the resilience of restored and potential restoration sites along future trajectories
 are lacking, yet they are necessary to inform restoration project prioritization. Improved
 understanding of hydrological and sedimentary processes is available to support such
 projections and the development of management alternatives accounting for basin and oceanic
 processes. The persistence of all CRE wetlands depends on future rates of sediment accretion
 and water level change. Wetland functions as habitats for juvenile salmonids and temperature
 refugia for migrating adults likewise depend on long-term trends. Yet the extrapolation from site-
 specific empirical models is insufficient to inform planners in a complex system. Furthermore, it
 is unclear whether future modifications to hydrosystem management practices in the basin and
 tributaries may impact the effectiveness of estuary and tidal river wetland restoration projects.
 The watershed processes in tributaries to the CRE are generally understudied, and individual
 tributary rivers appear to range widely in their potential to affect CRE salmon habitats.

 • Institute a gaging study of tributary flood regimes, in association with state and federal
 partners with experience in river gaging and data provision, coupled with studies of the
 affected plant communities and habitat functions for both juveniles and adults across the
 historical floodplain. This represents a shift of the predominant perspective in CRE
 physical sciences toward across-channel (lateral) processes of the estuary subbasin, in
 addition to the traditionally studied along-channel (longitudinal) processes.
 • With a high degree of spatial resolution, determine wetland accretion trends across the
 CRE system. Model future scenarios to evaluate the resilience of salmon habitats (rates
 of SLR versus sediment accretion) and determine the required rate of wetland accretion.
 Key questions to consider: Will existing wetlands require additional management? What
 design considerations need to be included to ensure the longevity of restorations?
 • Link wetland models to existing high-resolution hydrodynamic models to understand
 factors promoting sediment accretion by mechanistically connecting system-scale
 processes to wetland-scale processes. Ideally, employ an ensemble approach
 leveraging multiple existing models to increase certainty and enable predictions under
 different future scenarios.

 5.2.4 Quantitative Ecological Restoration Research and Modeling

 Few research studies have been designed to quantitatively establish relationships among
 biological and environmental factors – e.g., how wetland plant species composition and
 productivity affect invertebrate density and fish growth. If this data is available, statistical
 analyses can be used to describe or predict conditions. Ecological models can then be used to
 synthesize analytical findings to address decision needs. Without quantitative information about
 the mechanisms governing relationships between environmental and biological states,
 quantitative modeling will be limited.

 • Evaluate the use of existing CRE research and monitoring data to explore statistical
 relationships among habitat conditions and biological response variables. Key questions
 to consider: Are data available and accessible? What is the quality of the data? How are
 data currently used to support decision-making and determine success of restoration

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 70

 outcomes? If data do not support decision-making, why? What are the data gaps for
 estimating relationships between different environmental and/or biological states?
 • Depending on availability of existing data, strategically select existing and/or new
 monitored indicators that could best support decision-making, evaluate restoration
 outcomes, or understand future states given a set of expected conditions.
 • Quantify the uncertainty of monitored indicators to inform future monitoring and model
 development. The goal is not to resolve all sources of uncertainty. Instead, the goal is to
 understand how quantifying and, when possible, reducing uncertainty can improve
 decision-making.

 5.3 Programmatic Needs
 Over the past 25 years, CEERP’s investments in basic science have addressed critical
 uncertainties and advanced fundamental understanding of the CRE, providing an essential
 foundation for the next phase. The program should place greater emphasis on applying this
 knowledge through restoration actions, targeted effectiveness monitoring and research, and
 evaluation of progress toward salmon and ecosystem recovery. The following subsections
 identify four areas of potential programmatic advancement in program design, expectations,
 planning, and prioritization

 5.3.1 Aligning the Future RME Framework with the State of the Science

 Many of the advances in the state of the science on the CRE support decision-making, and
 future monitoring and research should be aligned with the current knowledge base. Much has
 been learned about heterogeneity of the system through examination of the variation across
 environmental and biological datasets. The CEERP research, monitoring and evaluation plan
 (Johnson et al. 2008) provided a framework for RME data collection and launched a then-new
 structure for identifying and aligning data collection around low, medium, and high levels of
 effort at given sites. This resulted in an increase in action effectiveness data – e.g., more than
 60 restoration sites were monitored between 2012−2025 (Table 4). However, published data
 from this review accounted for only 17 sites, and independent CEERP reviews highlight the
 need for focused investigations and experiments at selected sites (Table 6, Table 7).

 CEERP efforts associated with status and trends monitoring, restoration action effectiveness,
 and the use of reference sites have positively impacted understanding of the CRE ecosystem
 relative to juvenile salmon. Yet, the applications and aims established during the early program
 (Johnson et al. 2008; Diefenderfer et al. 2011b, 2016b) were not fully executed. For example,
 the statistical sampling designs for both the reference sites framework and the AEM framework
 assumed a more homogeneous spatiotemporal system than we now understand exists.
 Moreover, the program’s status and trends sampling design, which called for a rotational panel
 (Johnson et al. 2008), was not implemented, and the data collected from a limited number of
 fixed sites cannot be used to infer status or trends across the spatially variable CRE. Original
 designs did not fully account for the concept of ecological hysteresis (the fact that the post-
 restoration return path may not mirror the initial path of the system after diking), nor did they
 account for rapid environmental change since—both of which lead to a gap in the ability to
 predict restoration trajectories. The assumption that measurable attributes at restoration sites
 will re-enter the historical range as indicated by a suite of reference sites is unlikely to be true.
 On this basis, certain programmatic assumptions and approaches developed over two decades
 ago are no longer generally recommended based on this review.

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 71

 It is time to reframe the research, monitoring, and evaluation process from the standpoint of
 what has been learned through CEERP programs and related research.

 • Ramp up the study of restoration sites with hypothesis-driven research and monitoring
 on restoration sites. Any research that sponsors agree has the potential to inform
 restoration planning, design, engineering, and/or implementation methods should be
 recommended as part of an overall effort to support the implementation of restoration
 projects that achieve functional requirements as quickly as possible. Participation by site
 restoration managers is imperative to ensure that all observations are accounted for
 throughout study periods.
 • We recommend a broader discussion or workshop focused on reference and monitoring
 sites. The objectives should be aimed at determining whether previous research and
 monitoring can be leveraged to better understand future outcomes at restoration sites, or
 if the data from these locations is no longer relevant.
 • It is unclear whether continued monitoring of the typical suite of metrics is necessary.
 Despite multiple years of annual reporting, synthesis of these data was often lacking and
 comparative trends between sites and years were not always reported. Evaluate existing
 CEERP data to determine if new analyses can advance understanding of restoration
 outcomes, inform prioritization for future monitored metrics, or validate current metrics.
 • The next phase of CEERP would benefit from emphasizing selection of monitored
 indicators that are best suited for evaluating restoration response, testing hypotheses
 about restoration success, and informing future restoration planning and design. The
 governing factors for collecting data should focus on whether or how a given response
 variable will advance understanding of restoration science and outcomes in the CRE and
 less on levels.

 5.3.2 Updated Weighting for the Prioritization of Restoration Project Proposals

 This review indicated that several aspects of restoration proposal assessment may be worthy of
 reconsideration in light of the accumulated knowledge base. Recent ERTG reports, for example,
 indicate the importance of factors related to climate resilience for restoration project success,
 and together with other evidence suggest that the group’s weighting of proposed restoration
 projects be updated to reflect the current state of the science.

 Recommended actions. The following topics deserve reexamination or renewed attention to
 modernize assessments:
 • Incorporating future flow scenarios in combination with elevation (sediment accretion)
 scenarios in restoration project proposals would allow ERTG to better evaluate
 resilience. This could be accomplished using outputs from existing hydrodynamic
 models using regionally developed and accepted input data for climate and operations
 scenarios. It should be noted that uncertainty from future operations management
 agreements in the basin would remain.
 • The traditional approach to ecological restoration was to restore what was historically
 present; however, it has been recognized that the environment is not stationary, so this
 is not a realistic goal. The future focus of CRE restoration should be on habitat types that
 have had the greatest losses, but only to the extent that they are predicted to be resilient
 to a range (envelope) of potential future stressors. Structurally, the habitat with the

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 72

 greatest future ecological value is the one that will be sustainable within the envelope of
 future uncertainties in physical processes and ecosystem responses.
 • In the context of historical change, and given recent advancements in understanding of
 the relatively greater extent of tidal forests and scrub-shrub than marshes, encouraging
 swamp restoration goals is prudent in locations deemed to be resilient. This may be
 associated with development of restoration methods to accelerate successional change
 to woody plants and buffering for potential sea level rise and catastrophic coseismic
 subsidence of the floodplain through wetland migration areas.
 • Decisions about relative priorities made early in the program may have overly minimized
 the potential functions of tributaries to the estuary on floodplain habitats for salmon in
 both juvenile and adult life stages. More recent research on long-term trends in
 temperature, the food web, and sub-basin land cover change in the mainstem CRE
 versus its tributaries indicates an important role for tributary watersheds now and in the
 future, and these habitats should be reconsidered in prioritization schemes.

 5.3.3 Strategic Planning to Support Evaluation of Program Outcomes

 Periodic syntheses of knowledge are a vital part of program review. However, there appear to
 be opportunities for the program to consider whether the following would add value to the
 program adaptive management process: 1) increasing the frequency of evaluation; 2) reviewing
 the ease and accessibility of data to support syntheses; 3) developing an analytical framework
 to support quantitative analyses for the numerous projects occurring under CEERP; and 4)
 establishing criteria for understanding when success has been achieved (Bottom et al. 2025)
 and when monitoring can be discontinued (ISRP 2022). Coordination with program managers in
 adjacent and interacting geographic areas to share new syntheses is also necessary to advance
 mutual goals.

 • Determine what are the “off-ramps” for ongoing CEERP research studies. Could
 performance measures (Bottom et al. 2025) help to guide how research and monitoring
 is prioritized and the “sunset” of research as appropriate?
 • Recently, the AAs have begun to pilot a review process for CEERP research and
 monitoring projects, with ERTG members providing a central role as expert technical
 reviewers. We recommend institutionalizing a peer-reviewed process for research and
 monitoring within CEERP including prioritized research questions. A structured,
 independent peer-reviewed process should emphasize the need for robust study
 designs and analytical plans (Section 5.2.1) as well as research questions that prioritize
 advancing knowledge of restoration outcomes – e.g., measuring success, informing
 restoration design, aiding in decision-making. A review process would contribute
 independent perspectives on study limitations, which could be used to weigh limitations
 against benefits, particularly as related to program needs.
 • We recommend producing and regularly updating a brief two-page synthesis of key
 research questions that could be available on the CEERP website and serve as a simple
 reference guide for managers, restoration practitioners, and CEERP researchers. Such
 a reference would efficiently point to the current state of the science of physical
 processes, habitats, and salmon ecology in the CRE. They would also create an
 opportunity to benchmark future research ideas with existing knowledge.

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 73

 • CEERP program managers may find benefit from reevaluating and confirming various
 conditions that have previously been considered outside of the program scope – e.g.
 toxins, predation, hatchery fish.
 • It is unclear whether, as knowledge increases, CEERP managers will have influence on
 management practices that are external to the CEERP geographic footprint (whether in
 the CR interior basin or in estuary subbasin watersheds), but impact estuarine
 restoration outcomes studied by CEERP. Examples include flow and sediment effects on
 habitat availability, suitability, access, and resilience. Continuing engagement with
 agencies and practitioners working in ecosystems adjacent to the estuary or in the
 broader basin may help produce informed outcomes for regional co-managers.

 5.3.4 Data Preservation, Recovery, and Accessibility

 At the program level, it is prudent to leverage previously collected data. However, we found that
 the origins of data sets underpinning CEERP were incompletely catalogued, in part because the
 archiving policies of institutions contracted by the program have changed. This prevents access
 to the original materials, whether by the public or researchers, except exclusively for those who
 downloaded versions previously available online or received them from associated
 researcher(s). It also prevents the proper citation of key studies, such as the digitization and
 correction of U.S. government-produced maps of the 1800s CRE that was conducted from
 1998-2008; this CEERP-funded work has since been removed from University of Washington
 online repositories and is inconsistently cited in the literature.

 Furthermore, relevant research that occurred decades prior to the establishment of CEERP is
 now available online in the “gray literature,” but was not included in NMFS (2000) or in founding
 documents of CEERP such as Johnson et al. (2003, 2008). Such research, including aerial
 photo interpretation and field surveys, could enable an improved understanding of ecosystem
 trajectories, including the invasions of non-native species.

 Finally, a notable hurdle in this synthesis effort was the inaccessibility of data from the various
 monitoring and research programs. We encountered broken links or missing data from online
 sources and inconsistencies in how numerical data were summarized across studies and in
 data available online.

 • We recommend that all datasets produced by CEERP be developed according to the
 standards for metadata and format from well-regarded professional societies as required
 to be published online so that they can be accessed and cited by future researchers and
 managers. These standards have evolved and improved in recent years in partnerships
 between professional societies and scientific publishers and should be regarded as a
 reliable resource and repositories for CEERP.
 • To the extent needed for individual research questions with bearing on future restoration
 project design, it could be valuable to revisit newly uncovered archival data to extend
 statistical analysis of trends relative to environmental conditions and project future
 trends.
 • Recently, the AAs have publicly released a website for the CEERP. We suggest that the
 citations used in this synthesis be placed on the website as a searchable knowledge
 base to avoid losing continuity in understanding the CRE, as documented herein.

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 74

 5.4 Conclusion
 It is clear the contributions stemming from CEERP research activities have greatly advanced the
 understanding of the Columbia estuary ecosystem. Although we have gained knowledge, at this
 25-year milestone, we see there is an opportunity to increase the focus on understanding of
 post-restoration trajectories and juvenile salmon habitat functions. We recognize that
 management of an ecosystem involves continuous reassessment of multivariate information.
 Improvements could be made to research and monitoring occurring in the CRE, such as
 requiring robust sampling and analysis designs that address specific research questions and
 hypotheses. Collaboration with multiple stakeholders has been a strength. The program will
 continue to benefit from sustained collaboration because of the degree to which stressors from
 outside the CRE impact both fish and restoration project success.

Synthesis of Uncertainties, Needs, and Recommended Actions 75
